Dynamics of contact points in 2D Boussinesq flow Thanks: Y. Zheng was supported by National Natural Science Foundation of China with Grant No. 11901350 and No. 12371172.
Yunrui Zheng

Abstract
. We consider the evolution of contact lines for thermal convection of viscous fluids in a 2D open-top vessel. The domain is bounded above by a free moving boundary and otherwise by the solid wall of a vessel. The dynamics of the fluid are governed by the incompressible Boussinesq approximation under the influence of gravity, and the interface between fluid and air is under the effect of capillary forces. Motivated by energy-dissipation structure in [Guo-Tice, J. Eur. Math. Soc, 2024], we develop global well posedness theory in the framework of nonlinear energy methods for the initial data sufficiently close to equilibrium. Moreover, the solutions decay to equilibrium at an exponential rate. Our methods are mainly based on the construction of solutions to convected heat equation and a priori estimates of a geometric formulation of the Boussinesq equations.



2020 Mathematics Subject Classification
Primary 35Q30, 35R35, 76D45; Secondary 35B40, 76E17, 76E99

1.
Introduction

1.1.
Formulation of the Problem

We now consider the dynamical cooling process of hot water or coffee contained in 2D cup with adiabatic boundary. A
2
D open top vessel is treated as a bounded, connected open set
\mathcal{V}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2}
which consists of two disjoint sections, i.e.,
\mathcal{V}=\mathcal{V}_{top}\cup\mathcal{V}_{bot}
.
We refer to Figure 1 for an example.

\mathcal{V}_{top}
\mathcal{V}_{bot}

Figure 1.
A vessel
\mathcal{V}
.

\Omega(t)
\Sigma(t)
\Sigma_{s}(t)

Figure 2.
The domain
\Omega(t)
.

Assume that the “top” part
\mathcal{V}_{top}
is a rectangle channel

\mathcal{V}_{top}:=\mathcal{V}\cap\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}=\{y\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:-\ell<y_{1}<\ell,0\leq y_{2}<L\}

for some
\ell
,
L>0
, where
\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}
is the upper half plane
\mathbb{R}^{2}_{+}=\{y\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:y_{2}\geq 0\}
. Similarly, we write the “bottom” part as

\mathcal{V}_{bot}:=\mathcal{V}\cap\mathbb{R}^{2}_{-}=\mathcal{V}\cap\{y\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:y_{2}\leq 0\}.

Clearly,
I=(-\ell,\ell)\times\{0\}
. In addition, we also assume that the boundary
\partial\mathcal{V}
of
\mathcal{V}
is
C^{2}
away from the points
(\pm\ell,L)
.

Now we consider a viscous incompressible fluid filling
\mathcal{V}_{bot}
entirely and
\mathcal{V}_{top}
partially. More precisely, we assume that the fluid occupies the domain
\Omega(t)
,

\Omega(t):=\mathcal{V}_{bot}\cup\big\{y\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:-\ell<y_{1}<\ell,\ 0<y_{2}<\zeta(y_{1},t)\big\},

where the free surface
\zeta(y_{1},t)
is assumed to be a graph of the function
\zeta:[-\ell,\ell]\times\mathbb{R}_{+}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
satisfying
0<\zeta(\pm\ell,t)\leq L
for all
t\in\mathbb{R}_{+}
, which means the fluid does not spill out of the top domain. For simplicity, we write the free surface as
\Sigma(t)=\{y_{2}=\zeta(y_{1},t)\}
and the interface between fluid and solid as
\Sigma_{s}(t)=\partial\Omega(t)\backslash\Sigma(t)
.
We refer to Figure 2 for the description of domain.

For each
t>0
, the fluid is described by its velocity, pressure and temperature
(U,P,\Theta):\Omega(t)\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathbb{R}
which is governed by the incompressible Boussinesq approximation
[
4
]
:

(1.1)

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\partial_{t}U+U\cdot\nabla U+\nabla P-\mu\Delta U=-g\Theta e_{2}\quad&\text{in}&\ \Omega(t),\\
&\mathop{\rm div}\nolimits U=0\quad&\text{in}&\ \Omega(t),\\
&\partial_{t}\Theta+U\cdot\nabla\Theta-k\Delta\Theta=0\quad&\text{in}&\ \Omega(t),\end{aligned}\right.

where
\mu>0
is the coefficient of viscosity,
k>0
is the coefficient of heat conduction,
g>0
is the acceleration of gravity and
P=\bar{P}+gy_{2}-P_{atm}
by adjusting from the gravitational force and the constant atmospheric pressure
P_{atm}
to the actual pressure
\bar{P}
.

From the physical viewpoint, the fluid are supposed to satisfy some boundary conditions. On the free surface, the fluid should be kinematic
[
28
]
:

(1.2)

\left\{\begin{aligned} &S(P,U)\nu=g\zeta\nu-\sigma\mathcal{H}(\zeta)\nu\quad&\text{on}&\ \Sigma(t),\\
&\partial_{t}\zeta=U\cdot\nu\quad&\text{on}&\ \Sigma(t),\end{aligned}\right.

where
S(P,U)
is the viscous stress tensor

S(P,U):=PI-\mu\mathbb{D}U
,
with
I
the
2\times 2
identity matrix, and
\mathbb{D}U=\nabla U+\nabla^{T}U
the symmetric gradient of
U
. Here,
\mathcal{H}(\zeta)=\partial_{1}\left(\frac{\partial_{1}\zeta}{(1+|\partial_{1}\zeta|^{2})^{1/2}}\right)

is twice the mean curvature of the free surface.
\sigma=\sigma_{1}-\sigma_{2}\Theta
is the coefficient of surface tension, with
\sigma_{1}>0
and
\sigma_{2}\in\mathbb{R}
.
On the fixed boundary, the fluid satisfies the Navier-slip condition:

(1.3)

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\big(S(P,U)\nu-\beta U\big)\cdot\tau=0\quad&\text{on}&\ \Sigma_{s}(t),\\
&U\cdot\nu=0\quad&\text{on}&\ \Sigma_{s}(t).\end{aligned}\right.

Without loss of generality, we assume that the temperature of atmosphere around the fluid is
0
. Due to the adiabatic solid boundary, we assume that
\Theta=0
on
\Sigma_{s}(t)
. The heat on the free surface
\Sigma(t)
satisfies the Newton’s cooling law due to the heat conduction between fluid and air:

(1.4)

k\nu\cdot\nabla\Theta+\Theta=0.

The fluid on the contact points satisfies the condition:

(1.5)

\partial_{t}\zeta(\pm\ell,t)=\mathscr{V}\left([\![\gamma]\!]\mp\sigma\frac{\partial_{1}\zeta}{(1+|\partial_{1}\zeta|^{2})^{1/2}}(\pm\ell,t)\right),

where
\mathscr{V}:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
is the contact point velocity response function which is a
C^{2}
increasing diffeomorphism satisfying
\mathscr{V}(0)=0
.
[\![\gamma]\!]:=\gamma_{sv}-\gamma_{sf}
for
\gamma_{sv}
,
\gamma_{sf}\in\mathbb{R}
, where
\gamma_{sv}
,
\gamma_{sf}
are measures of the free-energy per unit length with respect to the solid-vapor and solid-fluid intersection. We also assume that the Young relation

|[\![\gamma]\!]|<\sigma
(
[
29
]
),
which is necessary for the equilibrium state we will consider in the following. For convenience, we introduce the inverse function
\mathscr{W}
of
\mathscr{V}
and rewrite (
1.5
) as

(1.6)

\mathscr{W}\big(\partial_{t}\zeta(\pm\ell,t)\big)=[\![\gamma]\!]\mp\sigma\frac{\partial_{1}\zeta}{(1+|\partial_{1}\zeta|^{2})^{1/2}}(\pm\ell,t),

which is the dynamic law at contact points.

The contact points in 2D or contact lines in 3D fluids, with temperature absent, has been studied for a long time, and we may refer to
[
6
, de Gennes]
for an overview. There are so many papers dealing with free boundary problems of contact points or contact lines, that we have to mention some works closely related to our background settings. For the contact lines in inviscid flow, we refer to
[
7
, T. de Poyferré]
and
[
20
, Ming-Wang]
for the breakthrough of water wave problem.

The analysis for Navier-slip conditions with contact angle can be referred to
[
21
, Ren-E]
and
[
5
, Cox]
.
When the contact angle is fixed by
\frac{\pi}{2}
, the well-posedness of 2D contact points problem in the Navier-Stokes flow is studied in
[
22
, Schweizer]
and the 3D contact line problem with an additional periodic direction is studied in
[
2
, Bodea]
.

For the non-stationary problem, the well-posedness and stability for 2D Navier-stokes flow are studied by
[
11
, Guo-Tice]
and
[
12
, Guo-Tice-Wu-Zheng]
. There are also many results for the stationary problem of contact points or lines. The solvability of stationary Navier-Stokes problem for dynamic contact angles and moving contact lines is studied by
[
17
, Kröner]
and
[
23
, Socolosky]
, respectively. The existence results of the Navier-Stokes equations for both static and dynamic contact points and lines in weighted Hölder spaces are proved by
[
24
,
25
, Solonnikov]
. The 3D contact lines for stationary Navier-Stokes equations with fixed angle
\frac{\pi}{2}
is studied by
[
14
, Jin]
. The local well-posedness and stability for the 2D contact points in the Stokes flow are studied by
[
10
, Guo-Tice]
and
[
27
, Tice-Zheng]

The contact angles are also closely related to the droplet problem. The stability problem for droplets governed by the Stokes equations is studied in
[
26
, Tice-Wu]
. The well-posedness for droplets governed by Darcy’s law is studied in
[
18
, Knüpfer-Masmoudi]
. Recently, the well-posedness of the Stokes equations on a wedge in weighted Sobolve spaces with Navier-slip boundary conditions is studied by
[
3
, Bravin-Gnann-Knüpfer-Masmoudi-Roodenburg-Sauer]
.

To the authors’ best knowledge, there is still no well-posedness and stability for the dynamic contact points problem with temperature at present. Our aim is to establish the first result for the global well-posedness for the 2D Boussinesq contact line problem. By referring to
[
10
, Guo-Tice]
or
[
8
, Finn]
, we known that (
1.1
), under the condition of Young relation, admits an equilibrium for
U=0
,
P=P_{0}
a constant,
\Theta=0
and
\zeta(y_{1},t)=\zeta_{0}(y_{1})
satisfies

(1.7)

P_{0}=g\zeta_{0}-\sigma_{1}\mathcal{H}(\zeta_{0})\quad\text{on}\ (-\ell,\ell),\quad\sigma_{1}\frac{\partial_{1}\zeta_{0}}{\sqrt{1+|\partial_{1}\zeta_{0}|^{2}}}(\pm\ell)=\pm[\![\gamma]\!].

This equilibrium would allow us to switch the free surface problem into a fixed domain one.
Let
\zeta_{0}\in C^{\infty}[-\ell,\ell]
be the equilibrium surface given by (
1.7
). We then define the equilibrium domain

\Omega:=\mathcal{V}_{b}\cup\big\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}|-\ell<x_{1}<\ell,0<x_{2}<\zeta_{0}(x_{1})\big\}\subset\mathbb{R}^{2}
.
The boundary
\partial\Omega
of the equilibrium
\Omega
is defined by

\partial\Omega:=\Sigma\cup\Sigma_{s}
,
where

\Sigma:=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}|-\ell<x_{1}<\ell,x_{2}=\zeta_{0}(x_{1})\}
and
\Sigma_{s}=\partial\Omega\backslash\Sigma
.
Here
\Sigma
is the equilibrium free surface. Denote the corner angle
\omega\in(0,\pi)
of
\Omega
formed by the fluid and solid.
We assume that the function
\zeta(y_{1},t)
of free surface is the perturbation of
\zeta_{0}(y_{1})
as

\zeta(y_{1},t)=\zeta_{0}(y_{1})+\eta(y_{1},t)
.
Let
\phi\in C^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
be such that
\phi(z)=0
for
z\leq\frac{1}{4}\min\zeta_{0}
and
\phi(z)=z
for
z\geq\frac{1}{2}\min\zeta_{0}
.
Now we define the mapping
\Phi:\Omega\mapsto\Omega(t)
by

(1.8)

\Phi(x_{1},x_{2},t):=\left(x_{1},x_{2}+\frac{\phi(x_{2})}{\zeta_{0}(x_{1})}\bar{\eta}(x_{1},x_{2},t)\right)=(y_{1},y_{2})\in\Omega(t),

with
\bar{\eta}(x_{1},x_{2},t):=\mathcal{P}E\eta(x_{1},x_{2}-\zeta_{0}(x_{1}),t)
,
where
E:H^{s}(-\ell,\ell)\mapsto H^{s+\frac{1}{2}}(\mathbb{R})
is a bounded extension operator for all
0\leq s\leq 3
and
\mathcal{P}
is the lower Poisson extension given by

\mathcal{P}f(x_{1},x_{2}):=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\hat{f}(\xi)e^{2\pi|\xi|x_{2}}e^{2\pi ix_{1}\xi}\,\mathrm{d}\xi.

If
\eta
is sufficiently small (in proper Sobolev spaces), the mapping
\Phi
is a
C^{1}
diffeomorphism of
\Omega
onto
\Omega(t)
that maps the components of
\partial\Omega
to the corresponding components of
\partial\Omega(t)
. The Jacobian matrix
\nabla\Phi
and the transform matrix
\mathcal{A}
of
\Phi
are:

(1.9)

\nabla\Phi=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&0\\
A&J\end{array}\right),\quad\mathcal{A}=(\nabla\Phi)^{-T}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cc}1&-AK\\
0&K\end{array}\right),

with entries

(1.10)

A:=W\partial_{1}\bar{\eta}-\frac{\phi}{\zeta_{0}^{2}}\partial_{1}\zeta_{0}\bar{\eta},\quad J:=1+\frac{\phi^{\prime}}{\zeta_{0}}\bar{\eta}+W\partial_{2}\bar{\eta},\quad K:=\frac{1}{J},\quad W:=\frac{\phi}{\zeta_{0}}.

We define the transform operators as follows.

(\nabla_{\mathcal{A}}f)_{i}:=\mathcal{A}_{ij}\partial_{j}f,\quad\mathop{\rm div}\nolimits_{\mathcal{A}}X:=\mathcal{A}_{ij}\partial_{j}X_{i},\quad\Delta_{\mathcal{A}}f:=\mathop{\rm div}\nolimits_{\mathcal{A}}\nabla_{\mathcal{A}}f

for appropriate
f
and
X
. We write the stress tensor

S_{\mathcal{A}}(P,u):=PI-\mu\mathbb{D}_{\mathcal{A}}u

where
I
is the
2\times 2
identity matrix and
(\mathbb{D}_{\mathcal{A}}u)_{ij}:=\mathcal{A}_{ik}\partial_{k}u_{j}+\mathcal{A}_{jk}\partial_{k}u_{i}
is the symmetric
\mathcal{A}
–gradient. Note that if we extend
\mathop{\rm div}\nolimits_{\mathcal{A}}
to act on symmetric tensors in the natural way, then
\mathop{\rm div}\nolimits_{\mathcal{A}}S_{\mathcal{A}}(P,u)=-\mu\Delta_{\mathcal{A}}u+\nabla_{\mathcal{A}}P
for vector fields satisfying
\mathop{\rm div}\nolimits_{\mathcal{A}}u=0
.

Notice that
\Phi
is a diffeomorphism (see
[
27
]
), so that we rewrite the solutions to (
1.1
) as a perturbation around the equilibrium state
(U,P,\Theta,\zeta)=(0,P_{0},0,\zeta_{0})
. By defining new perturbed unknowns
(u,p,\theta,\eta)
via
U=0+u
,
P=P_{0}+p
,
\Theta=0+\theta
and
\zeta=\zeta_{0}+\eta
, we can use the analysis of
[
11
]
to transform the problem (
1.1
) into the perturbation form:

(1.11)

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\partial_{t}u-\partial_{t}\bar{\eta}WK\partial_{2}u+u\cdot\nabla_{\mathcal{A}}u+\mathop{\rm div}\nolimits_{\mathcal{A}}S_{\mathcal{A}}(p,u)=-g\theta e_{2}\quad&\text{in}&\ \Omega,\\
&\mathop{\rm div}\nolimits_{\mathcal{A}}u=0\quad&\text{in}&\ \Omega,\\
&\partial_{t}\theta-\partial_{t}\bar{\eta}WK\partial_{2}\theta+u\cdot\nabla_{\mathcal{A}}\theta-k\Delta_{\mathcal{A}}\theta=0\quad&\text{in}&\ \Omega,\end{aligned}\right.

under the boundary conditions

(1.12)

\left\{\begin{aligned} &S_{\mathcal{A}}(p,u)\mathcal{N}=\left[g\eta-(\sigma_{1}-\sigma_{2}\theta)\partial_{1}\left(\frac{\partial_{1}\eta}{(1+|\partial_{1}\zeta_{0}|)^{3/2}}\right)-\sigma\partial_{1}\Big(\mathcal{R}(\partial_{1}\zeta_{0},\partial_{1}\eta)\Big)\right]\mathcal{N}\ &\text{on}&\ \Sigma,\\
&(S_{\mathcal{A}}(p,u)\nu-\beta u)\cdot\tau=0\ &\text{on}&\ \Sigma_{s},\\
&u\cdot\nu=0\ &\text{on}&\ \Sigma_{s},\\
&\theta=0\ &\text{on}&\ \Sigma_{s},\\
&\mathcal{N}\cdot\nabla\theta+\theta|\mathcal{N}|=0\ &\text{on}&\ \Sigma\\
&\partial_{t}\eta=u\cdot\mathcal{N}\ &\text{on}&\ \Sigma,\\
&\kappa\partial_{t}\eta+\kappa\hat{\mathscr{W}}(\partial_{t}\eta)=\mp\sigma\left(\frac{\partial_{1}\eta}{(1+|\zeta_{0}|^{2})^{3/2}}+\mathcal{R}(\partial_{1}\zeta_{0},\partial_{1}\eta)\right)\ &\text{at}&\ \pm\ell,\end{aligned}\right.

where
\kappa:=\mathscr{W}^{\prime}(0)>0
,
\mathcal{A}
,
\mathcal{N}:=-\partial_{1}\zeta e_{1}+e_{2}
are still determined in terms of
\zeta=\zeta_{0}+\eta
and
\mathcal{R}(\partial_{1}\zeta_{0},\partial_{1}\eta)
is the Taylor expansion remainder of
\frac{1}{\sqrt{1+|\partial_{1}\zeta|^{2}}}
with respect to
\zeta_{0}
multiplied by
\partial_{1}\eta
. In the following, let
\mathcal{N}_{0}
be the non-unit normal vector for the equilibrium surface
\Sigma
, and
\mathcal{N}=\mathcal{N}_{0}-\partial_{1}\eta e_{1}
. The importance of
\varepsilon
is discussed in Section
1.3
.
Since all differential operators in (
1.11
) are in terms of
\eta
, (
1.11
) is connected to the geometry of the free surface. This geometric structure is essential to control higher-order derivatives.

1.2.
Main Theorem

We denote
\omega_{eq}
as the equilibrium contact angle (refer to
[
11
]
) and the parameter
\varepsilon_{max}:=\min\{1,-1+\pi/\omega\}
such that for
0<\varepsilon\leq\varepsilon_{max}<1
,
there exist three parameters
\alpha
,
\varepsilon_{-}
and
\varepsilon_{+}
that satisfy

(1.13)

\displaystyle 0<\alpha<\varepsilon_{-}<\varepsilon_{+}<\varepsilon_{max},\quad\alpha<\min\left\{\frac{\varepsilon_{-}}{2},\frac{\varepsilon_{+}-\varepsilon_{-}}{2}\right\},\quad\varepsilon_{+}\leq\frac{\varepsilon_{-}+1}{2},

\displaystyle q_{-}:=\frac{2}{2-\varepsilon_{-}}<q_{+}:=\frac{2}{2-\varepsilon_{+}}<q_{max}:=\frac{2}{2-\varepsilon_{max}}.

Define the energy

(1.14)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(u,p,\eta)

\displaystyle:=\|u\|_{W^{2,q_{+}}}^{2}+\|\partial_{t}u\|_{1+\varepsilon_{-}/2}^{2}+\sum_{k=0}^{2}\|\partial_{t}^{k}u\|_{0}^{2}+\|p\|_{W^{1,q_{+}}}^{2}+\|\partial_{t}p\|_{0}^{2}+\|\eta\|_{W^{3-1/q_{+},q_{+}}}^{2}

\displaystyle+\|\partial_{t}\eta\|_{3/2+(\varepsilon_{-}-\alpha)/2}^{2}+\sum_{j=0}^{2}\|\partial_{t}^{j}\eta\|_{1}^{2},

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(\theta)

\displaystyle:=\|\theta\|_{W^{2,q_{+}}}^{2}+\|\partial_{t}\theta\|_{1+\varepsilon_{-}/2}^{2}+\sum_{k=0}^{2}\|\partial_{t}^{k}\theta\|_{0}^{2},

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}(t)

\displaystyle:=\mathcal{E}(u,p,\eta,\theta)=\mathcal{E}(u,p,\eta)+\mathcal{E}(\theta),

and the dissipation

(1.15)

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}(u,p,\eta)

\displaystyle:=\|u\|_{W^{2,q_{+}}}^{2}+\|\partial_{t}u\|_{W^{2,q_{-}}}^{2}+\sum_{j=0}^{2}\Big(\|\partial_{t}^{j}u\|_{1}^{2}+\|\partial_{t}^{j}u\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma_{s})}^{2}\Big)+\|p\|_{W^{1,q_{+}}}^{2}+\|\partial_{t}p\|_{W^{1,q_{-}}}^{2}

\displaystyle+\|\eta\|_{W^{3-1/q_{+},q_{+}}}^{2}+\|\partial_{t}\eta\|_{W^{3-1/q_{-},q_{-}}}^{2}+\sum_{j=0}^{2}\Big(\|\partial_{t}^{j}\eta\|_{3/2-\alpha}^{2}+[\partial_{t}^{j+1}\eta]_{\ell}^{2}\Big)+\|\partial_{t}^{3}\eta\|_{1/2-\alpha}^{2},

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}(\theta)

\displaystyle:=\|\theta\|_{W^{2,q_{+}}}^{2}+\|\partial_{t}\theta\|_{W^{2,q_{-}}}^{2}+\sum_{j=0}^{2}\Big(\|\partial_{t}^{j}\theta\|_{1}^{2}+\|\partial_{t}^{j}\theta\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma)}^{2}\Big),

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}(t)

\displaystyle:=\mathcal{D}(u,p,\eta,\theta)=\mathcal{D}(u,p,\eta)+\mathcal{D}(\theta).

Theorem 1.1
.

Assume that the contact angle
\omega_{eq}\in(0,\pi)
and
\varepsilon_{max}=\min\{1,-1+\pi/\omega_{eq}\}\in(0,1)
.
Suppose that the initial data
\Big(u_{0},p_{0},\theta_{0},\eta_{0},\partial_{t}u(0),\partial_{t}p(0),\partial_{t}\theta(0),\partial_{t}\eta(0),\partial_{t}^{2}u(0),\partial_{t}^{2}\theta(0),\partial_{t}^{2}\eta(0)\Big)
satisfy the compatibility conditions for
j=0,1,2

(1.16)

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\mathop{\rm div}\nolimits_{\mathcal{A}_{0}}D_{t}^{j}u(0)=0\quad&\text{in}&\ \Omega,\\
&D_{t}^{j}u(0)\cdot\nu=0\quad&\text{on}&\ \Sigma_{s},\\
&D_{t}^{j}u(0)\cdot\mathcal{N}(0)=\partial_{t}^{j+1}\eta(0)\quad&\text{on}&\ \Sigma,\end{aligned}\right.

and zero-average condition for
k=0,1,2

(1.17)

\int_{-\ell}^{\ell}\partial_{t}^{k}\eta(0)=0.

Then there exists sufficiently small
\delta_{0}>0
, such that if
\sqrt{\mathcal{E}(0)}+\|\partial_{t}^{2}\eta(0)\|_{W^{2-1/q_{+},q_{+}}}\leq\delta_{0}
, then there exists a unique solution
(u,p,\theta,\eta)
to (
1.11
) for
t\in[0,\infty)
that achieves the initial data and satisfies

(1.18)

\displaystyle\sup_{t\geq\infty}e^{\lambda t}\mathcal{E}(t)+\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathcal{D}(t)\,\mathrm{d}t\leq C\mathcal{E}(0)

for some universal constants
C>0
and
\lambda>0
.

Remark 1.1
.

Note that the term
\|\partial_{t}^{2}\eta(0)\|_{W^{2-1/q_{+},q_{+}}}^{2}
is stronger than the initial energy functional. This term is sufficient to guarantee that our initial data set is non-empty (see the discussion in Appendix
B
). Since
\Phi
is a
C^{1}
diffeomorphism due to
J>0
for
\eta
as well as its derivatives sufficiently small, we might change coordinates from
\Omega
to
\Omega(t)
to obtain solutions to (
1.1
).

Remark 1.2
.

The differential operator
D_{t}
in (
1.16
) is defined as
D_{t}u=\partial_{t}u-Ru
, where
R
is the transport of
-\partial_{t}JKI_{2\times 2}-\partial_{t}\mathcal{A}\mathcal{A}^{-1}
.
D_{t}
is introduced to preserve the divergence condition, i.e.,
\mathop{\rm div}\nolimits_{\mathcal{A}}(D_{t}^{j}u)=0
. (
D_{t}^{0}
should be considered as the identity.)

1.3.
Strategy of the proof

Our proof is mainly focused on the linear heat system

(1.19)

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\partial_{t}\theta-k\Delta_{\mathcal{A}}\theta=F^{8}\quad&\text{in}&\quad\Omega,\\
&k\mathcal{N}\cdot\nabla_{\mathcal{A}}\theta+\theta|\mathcal{N}|=F^{9}\quad&\text{on}&\quad\Sigma,\\
&\theta=0\quad&\text{on}&\quad\Sigma_{s},\end{aligned}\right.

coupled with the linear
\varepsilon-
regularized Navier-Stokes system

(1.20)

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\partial_{t}u+\mathop{\rm div}\nolimits_{\mathcal{A}}S_{\mathcal{A}}(p,u)+g\theta e_{2}=F^{1}\quad&\text{in}&\quad\Omega,\\
&\mathop{\rm div}\nolimits_{\mathcal{A}}u=0\quad&\text{in}&\quad\Omega,\\
&S_{\mathcal{A}}(p,u)\mathcal{N}=\left(\mathcal{K}(\eta+\varepsilon\eta_{t})-\sigma_{1}\partial_{1}F^{3}\right)\mathcal{N}+F^{4}\quad&\text{on}&\quad\Sigma,\\
&u\cdot\mathcal{N}=\partial_{t}\eta\quad&\text{on}&\quad\Sigma,\\
&(S_{\mathcal{A}}(p,u)\nu-\beta u)\cdot\tau=F^{5}\quad&\text{on}&\quad\Sigma_{s},\\
&u\cdot\nu=0\quad&\text{on}&\quad\Sigma_{s},\\
&\mp\sigma_{1}\frac{\partial_{1}(\eta+\varepsilon\eta_{t})}{(1+|\partial_{1}\zeta_{0}|^{2})^{3/2}}(\pm\ell)=\kappa(u\cdot\mathcal{N})(\pm\ell)\pm\sigma F^{3}(\pm\ell)-F^{7}(\pm\ell).\end{aligned}\right.

The role of
\varepsilon
is played to lift the regularity of
\eta
, borrowed the idea of
[
13
]
. By analyze the elliptic theory near the corner points of Poisson equation, we can establish the existence and uniqueness of (
1.19
). Then by plugging the results into the well-posedness of (
1.20
), we can establish the results of linear systems that are used to do the iteration to establish the well-posedness of nonlinear systems (
1.11
) and (
1.12
).

The elliptic theory for Poisson equation is determined via the spectral analysis of operator pencils. Then the symmtric structure of linear
\mathcal{A}
–heat equations allows us to use the Galerkin method to construct solutions via the two–tie energy methods. The first step is to construct the
(\theta,\partial_{t}\theta)
; the second step is to construct
\partial_{t}^{2}\theta
by
(\theta,\partial_{t}\theta)
constructed in the first step. In the second step, due to the loss of high regularity of
\partial_{t}\theta
, we have to introduce another iteration to control the interaction between
\nabla^{2}\partial_{t}^{2}\bar{\eta}
and
\partial_{t}\theta
.

The linear theory of (
1.20
) is similar to
[
12
]
after the minor modification of
\theta
and coefficient of surface tension. Because of the smallness of
\theta\partial_{1}\left(\frac{\partial_{1}\eta}{\sqrt{1+|\partial_{1}\eta|^{1}}}\right)
, the analysis in
[
12
]
is still correct besides these modifications. Based on the linear theory, we use the fixed point theory to establish the global well-posedness of the nonlinear
\varepsilon
–regularized system

(1.21)

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\partial_{t}u-\partial_{t}\bar{\eta}WK\partial_{2}u+u\cdot\nabla_{\mathcal{A}}u+\mathop{\rm div}\nolimits_{\mathcal{A}}S_{\mathcal{A}}(p,u)=-g\theta e_{2}\quad&\text{in}&\ \Omega,\\
&\mathop{\rm div}\nolimits_{\mathcal{A}}u=0\quad&\text{in}&\ \Omega,\\
&\partial_{t}\theta-\partial_{t}\bar{\eta}WK\partial_{2}\theta+u\cdot\nabla_{\mathcal{A}}\theta-k\Delta_{\mathcal{A}}\theta=0\quad&\text{in}&\ \Omega,\\
&S_{\mathcal{A}}(p,u)\mathcal{N}=\left[g\eta-(\sigma_{1}-\sigma_{2}\theta)\partial_{1}\left(\frac{\partial_{1}\eta+\varepsilon\partial_{1}\partial_{t}\eta}{(1+|\partial_{1}\zeta_{0}|)^{3/2}}\right)-\sigma\partial_{1}\Big(\mathcal{R}(\partial_{1}\zeta_{0},\partial_{1}\eta)\Big)\right]\mathcal{N}\ &\text{on}&\ \Sigma,\\
&(S_{\mathcal{A}}(p,u)\nu-\beta u)\cdot\tau=0\ &\text{on}&\ \Sigma_{s},\\
&u\cdot\nu=0\ &\text{on}&\ \Sigma_{s},\\
&\theta=0\ &\text{on}&\ \Sigma_{s},\\
&k\mathcal{N}\cdot\nabla\theta+\theta|\mathcal{N}|=0\ &\text{on}&\ \Sigma\\
&\partial_{t}\eta=u\cdot\mathcal{N}\ &\text{on}&\ \Sigma,\\
&\kappa\partial_{t}\eta+\kappa\hat{\mathscr{W}}(\partial_{t}\eta)=\mp\sigma_{1}\left(\frac{\partial_{1}\eta+\varepsilon\partial_{1}\partial_{t}\eta}{(1+|\zeta_{0}|^{2})^{3/2}}+\mathcal{R}(\partial_{1}\zeta_{0},\partial_{1}\eta)\right)\ &\text{at}&\ \pm\ell,\end{aligned}\right.

We define

(1.22)

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}^{\varepsilon}:=\mathcal{E}(u^{\varepsilon},p^{\varepsilon},\eta^{\varepsilon},\theta^{\varepsilon})+\varepsilon^{2}\|\partial_{t}\eta^{\varepsilon}\|_{W^{3-1/q_{+},q_{+}}}^{2}+\varepsilon\sum_{j=0}^{2}\|\partial_{t}^{j}\eta^{\varepsilon}\|_{H^{3/2-\alpha}}^{2}

(1.23)

\displaystyle\mathcal{D}^{\varepsilon}:=\mathcal{D}(u^{\varepsilon},p^{\varepsilon},\eta^{\varepsilon},\theta^{\varepsilon})+\varepsilon^{2}\|\partial_{t}\eta^{\varepsilon}\|_{W^{3-1/q_{+},q_{+}}}^{2}+\varepsilon^{2}\|\partial_{t}^{2}\eta^{\varepsilon}\|_{W^{3-1/q_{-},q_{-}}}^{2}+\varepsilon\sum_{j=0}^{2}\|\partial_{t}^{j}\eta^{\varepsilon}\|_{H^{1}}^{2},

where
\mathcal{E}
is the same as (
1.14
) and
\mathcal{D}
is the same as (
1.15
). Now the energy and dissipation do not propagate the regularity, we cannot use the usual way (for instance, set
t=0
for the first equation in (
1.21
)) to construct the energy of initial data. Now we apply the backward way to construct
\mathcal{E}^{\varepsilon}(0)
. Then we can establish the global well-posedness and stability for (
1.21
):

Theorem 1.2
.

Suppose that there exists a universal constant
\delta\in(0,1)
such that if a solution to (
5.1
) exists on the time interval
[0,T)
for
0<T\leq\infty
and obeys the estimate

\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\mathcal{E}^{\varepsilon}(t)\leq\delta_{0}
,
Then there exists universal constants
C>0
and
\lambda>0
such that

(1.24)

\displaystyle\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}e^{\lambda t}\mathcal{E}^{\varepsilon}(t)+\int_{0}^{T}\mathcal{D}^{\varepsilon}(t)\,\mathrm{d}t\leq C\mathcal{E}^{\varepsilon}(0).

Theorem
1.2
allows us to pass the limit
\varepsilon\to 0^{+}
to show that
\mathcal{E}^{\varepsilon}\to\mathcal{E}
in
L^{\infty}
and
\int_{0}^{T}\mathcal{D}^{\varepsilon}\to\int_{0}^{T}\mathcal{D}
. The convergence of initial energy
\mathcal{E}^{\varepsilon}(0)\to\mathcal{E}(0)
is given in Appendix
B
. In the construction of
\mathcal{E}(0)
, we still use the backward procedure, because the energy is not propagated in time.

1.4.
Constants and Sobolev Norms

Let
C>0
denote a universal constant that only depends on the parameters of the problem,
N
and
\Omega
, but does not depend on the data, etc. They are allowed to change from line to line. We will write
C=C(z)
to indicate that the constant
C
depends on
z
. And we will write
a\lesssim b
if
a\leq Cb
for a universal constant
C>0
.

We will write
H^{k}
instead of
H^{k}(\Omega)
,
W^{k,q}
instead of
W^{k,q}(\Omega)
for
k\geq 0
, and
H^{s}(\Sigma)
or
W^{s,q}(\Sigma)
with
s\in\mathbb{R}
for usual Sobolev spaces. Typically, we will write
H^{0}=L^{2}
, with the exception to this is we will use
L^{2}([0,T];H^{k})
(or
L^{2}([0,T];H^{s}(\Sigma))
) to denote the space of temporal square–integrable functions with values in
H^{k}
(or
H^{s}(\Sigma)
). Similarly, we write
L^{2}([0,T];W^{k,q})
or
L^{2}([0,T];W^{s,q})
.

Sometimes we will write
\|\cdot\|_{k}
instead of
\|\cdot\|_{H^{k}(\Omega)}
or
\|\cdot\|_{H^{k}(\Sigma)}
. We assume that functions have natural spaces. For example, the functions
u
,
p
and
\bar{\eta}
live on
\Omega
, while
\eta
lives on
\Sigma
. So we may write
\|\cdot\|_{H^{k}}
for the norms of
u
,
p
and
\bar{\eta}
in
\Omega
, and
\|\cdot\|_{H^{s}}
for norms of
\eta
on
\Sigma
.

Define time-independent spaces

(1.25)

{}_{0}H^{1}(\Omega):=\Big\{\theta\in H^{1}(\Omega)\Big|u=0\ \text{on}\ \Sigma_{s}\Big\},

endowed with the usual
H^{1}
norm,

(1.26)

\mathring{H}^{k}(U):=\left\{f\in H^{k}(U)\Big|\int_{U}f=0\right\},

where
U=\Omega
or
(-\ell,\ell)
and
k\in\mathbb{N}
.

Suppose that
\eta
is given and that
\mathcal{A}
,
J
and
\mathcal{N}
, etc are determined in terms of
\eta
. Let us define

(1.27)

[a,b]_{\ell}:=\kappa\Big(a(\ell)b(\ell)+a(-\ell)b(-\ell)\Big).

We denote
\|\xi\|_{1,\Sigma}:=\sqrt{(\xi,\xi)_{1,\Sigma}}
and
[\phi]_{\ell}:=\sqrt{[\phi,\phi]_{\ell}}
.

For convenience, let us define some time-dependent spaces

(1.28)

\mathcal{H}^{0}:=\Big\{\theta:\Omega\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\Big|\sqrt{J}\theta\in H^{0}(\Omega)\Big\}

endowed with the norm
\|\theta\|_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}:=\|\sqrt{J}\theta\|_{H^{0}}
,

(1.29)

\mathcal{H}^{1}:=\Big\{\theta:\Omega\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\Big|\|\theta\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}}<\infty,\ \theta=0\ \text{on}\ \Sigma_{s}\Big\}

endowed with the norm
\|\theta\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}}:=\|\nabla_{\mathcal{A}}\theta\|_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}+\|\theta\sqrt{|\mathcal{N}|}\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma)}
, where the
\mathcal{H}^{1}
-inner product is defined via
(\theta,\psi)_{\mathcal{H}^{1}}=(\nabla_{\mathcal{A}}\theta,\nabla_{\mathcal{A}}\psi)_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}+(\theta|\mathcal{N}|,\psi)_{L^{2}(\Sigma)}
.
Finally, we define the inner product on
L^{2}([0,T];\mathcal{H}^{1})
as

(\theta,\psi)_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{T}}=\int_{0}^{T}\Big(\theta(t),\psi\Big)_{\mathcal{H}^{1}}\,\mathrm{d}t
,
endowed with norm
\|\theta\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{T}}:=\sqrt{(\theta,\theta)_{\mathcal{H}^{1}_{T}}}
.

The following lemma implies that
\mathcal{H}^{1}
is equivalent to
{}_{0}H^{1}(\Omega)
, which is a modification of
[
9
]
.

Lemma 1.3
.

There exists a small universal
\delta_{0}>0
such that if
\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\|\eta(t)\|_{W^{3-1/q_{+},q_{+}}}<\delta_{0}
,
then

\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\|\theta\|_{k}\leq\|\theta\|_{\mathcal{H}^{k}}\leq\sqrt{2}\|\theta\|_{k}

for
k=0,1
and for all
t\in[0,T]
. As a consequence, for
k=0,1
,

\|\theta\|_{L^{2}H^{k}}\leq\|\theta\|_{\mathcal{H}^{k}_{T}}\leq\|\theta\|_{L^{2}H^{k}}
.

2.
Elliptic Theory for The Corner Domains

We now consider some elliptic estimates for Poisson equations with corner points.

2.1.
Analysis for Poisson equations near the corner points

Let
(r,\rho)
be the polar coordinates for
\mathbb{R}^{2}
.

K_{\omega}=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:r>0\ \text{and}\ \rho\in(-\pi/2,-\pi/2+\omega)\}

denotes the cone with open angle
\omega\in(0,\pi)
. The lower and upper boundaries of
K_{\omega}
are

\Gamma_{-}=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:r>0\ \text{and}\ \rho=-\pi/2\}\ \text{and}\ \Gamma_{+}=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{2}:r>0\ \text{and}\ \rho=-\pi/2+\omega\}

respectively.

Now, we consider the
\mathfrak{A}
-equation
We first give the proof of
\vartheta
through the Poisson equations

(2.1)

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} &-k\Delta_{\mathfrak{A}}\vartheta=G^{8}\quad&\text{in}&\quad K_{\omega},\\
&k\nabla_{\mathfrak{A}}\vartheta\cdot(\mathfrak{A}\nu)=G^{9}\quad&\text{on}&\quad\Gamma_{+},\\
&\vartheta=0\quad&\text{on}&\quad\Gamma_{-},\end{aligned}\right.

where the differential operators
\nabla_{\mathfrak{A}}
and
\Delta_{\mathfrak{A}}
are defined in the same way as
\nabla_{\mathcal{A}}
and
\Delta_{\mathcal{A}}
. Clearly, when
\mathfrak{A}=I_{2\times 2}
, (
2.1
) becomes

(2.2)

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} &-k\Delta\vartheta=G^{8}\quad&\text{in}&\quad K_{\omega},\\
&k\nabla\vartheta\cdot\nu=G^{9}\quad&\text{on}&\quad\Gamma_{+},\\
&\vartheta=0\quad&\text{on}&\quad\Gamma_{-}.\end{aligned}\right.

Theorem 2.1
.

Let
\delta=\pm
. Assume that the forcing terms in (
2.1
) satisfy
G^{3}\in L^{q_{\delta}}(K_{\omega})
and
G^{5}\in W^{1-1/q_{\delta},q_{\delta}}(\Gamma_{+})
.
Suppose that
\vartheta\in H^{1}(K_{\omega})
satisfies

\int_{K_{\omega}}k\nabla_{\mathfrak{A}}\vartheta\cdot\nabla_{\mathfrak{A}}\phi+\int_{\Gamma_{+}}\vartheta\phi=\int_{K_{\omega}}G^{8}\phi+\int_{\Gamma_{+}}G^{9}\phi,

for all
\phi\in H^{1}(K_{\omega})
. Furthermore, assume that
\vartheta
and all the forcing terms
G^{i}
are supported in
\bar{K}_{\omega}\cap B_{1}(0)
, where
B_{1}(0)
is a unit disk centered at
0
with radius
1
. Then
\nabla^{2}\vartheta\in L^{q_{\delta}}(K_{\omega})
. Moreover,

(2.3)

\|\nabla^{2}\vartheta\|_{L^{q_{\delta}}(K_{\omega})}^{2}\lesssim\|G^{8}\|_{L^{q_{\delta}}(K_{\omega})}^{2}+\|G^{9}\|_{W^{1-1/q_{\delta},q_{\delta}}(\Gamma)}^{2}.

Proof.

The key point for proof is the utilization of operator pencil (we refer to
[
16
]
for the definition). The eigenvalues of operator pencil for (
2.2
) could be easily derived (for instance, refer to
[
15
]
) as
\{\frac{n\pi}{\omega}:n\in\mathbb{Z}\setminus\{0\}\}
, which are not contained in

\{\lambda\in\mathbb{C}:0\leq\Re{\lambda}<1\},

which allows us to use the
W^{2,q_{\delta}}
estimate.
q_{\delta}<2
is determined by the argument of Stokes equations in the cone, indicated by
[
11
]
.
So we can use
[
15
, Theorem 8.2.1]
, which gives the condition in terms of eigenvalue of associated operator pencil for solving the elliptic system (
2.2
),
and then argue as
[
19
, Theorem 6.4.6]
to derive that
\nabla^{2}\vartheta\in L^{q_{\delta}}(K_{\omega})
satisfying

(2.4)

\displaystyle\|\nabla^{2}\vartheta\|_{L^{q_{\delta}}(K_{\omega})}^{2}\lesssim\|G^{8}\|_{L^{q_{\delta}}(K_{\omega})}^{2}+\|G^{9}\|_{W^{1-1/q_{\delta},1/q_{\delta}}(\Gamma_{+})}^{2}+\|\vartheta\|_{H^{1}(K_{\omega})}^{2}.

The assumptions on
\mathfrak{A}
guarantee that the two Poisson equations in (
2.1
) and (
2.2
) generate the same operator pencil, which implies that the solution
\vartheta
to (
2.1
) also satisfies (
2.4
).
From the assumption on
\vartheta
and the Poincáre inequality together with Sobolev embedding theory
we also have

(2.5)

\displaystyle\|\vartheta\|_{H^{1}(K_{\omega})}^{2}\lesssim\|G^{8}\|_{L^{q_{\delta}}(K_{\omega})}^{2}+\|G^{9}\|_{W^{1-1/q_{\delta},1/q_{\delta}}(\Gamma_{+})}^{2}.

The result is completed by combining (
2.4
) and (
2.5
).
∎

2.2.
\mathcal{A}
-Poisson equation

We first consider the strong solutions to Poisson equations

(2.6)

\left\{\begin{aligned} &-k\Delta\vartheta=G^{8}\quad&\text{in}&\quad\Omega,\\
&k\nabla\vartheta\cdot\nu=G^{9}&\text{on}&\quad\Sigma,\\
&\vartheta=0&\text{on}&\quad\Sigma_{s},\end{aligned}\right.

where
\nu
is the unit outward normal and unit tangential of
\psi\Omega
.

Definition 2.1
.

Assume that
G^{8}\in L^{q_{\delta}}(\Omega)
and
G^{9}\in W^{1-1/q_{\delta},q_{\delta}}(\Sigma)
for
\delta=\pm
. We say
\vartheta\in{}_{0}H^{1}(\Omega)
is a weak solution of (
2.6
) if

(2.7)

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}k\nabla\vartheta\cdot\nabla\phi=\int_{\Omega}G^{8}\phi+\int_{\Sigma}G^{9}\phi

holds for any
\phi\in{}_{0}H^{1}(\Omega)
. Clearly, from Sobolev embedding theory, the integrations on the righthand side of (
2.7
) is well-defined.

Now we give the sketch proof of existence and uniqueness of weak solutions of elliptic equation (
2.6
). First, (
2.7
) allows us to use Riesz representation theorem to obtain the existence of
\vartheta
such that
\|\nabla\vartheta\|_{0}^{2}\lesssim\|G^{8}\|_{L^{q_{\delta}}}^{2}+\|G^{9}\|_{W^{1-1/q_{\delta},q_{\delta}}}^{2}
. Then the Poincáre inequality implies
\|\vartheta\|_{1}^{2}\lesssim\|G^{8}\|_{L^{q_{\delta}}}^{2}+\|G^{9}\|_{W^{1-1/q_{\delta},q_{\delta}}}^{2}
.

The next theorem shows that, regularity of weak solution of the equation (
2.6
) could be improved to second-order.

Theorem 2.2
.

Assume that
G^{8}\in L^{q_{\delta}}(\Omega)
and
G^{9}\in W^{1-1/q_{\delta},q_{\delta}}(\Sigma)
for
\delta=\pm
. Then there exists a unique
\vartheta
solving (
2.6
) such that
\vartheta\in W^{2,q_{\delta}}(\Omega)
. Moreover,

(2.8)

\displaystyle\|\vartheta\|_{W^{2,q_{\delta}}(\Omega)}^{2}\lesssim\|G^{8}\|_{L^{q_{\delta}}(\Omega)}^{2}+\|G^{9}\|_{W^{1-1/q_{\delta},q_{\delta}}(\Sigma)}^{2}.

Proof.

The idea of this proof is very standard. We divided
\Omega
into three parts. One is away from the corners. Thus, we might use the standard elliptic theory (for instance,
[
1
, Theorem 10.5]
) to obtain the results. The other two parts are near corners. Then we might use Theorem
2.1
to derive the conclusion. The proof is in a similar way as
[
10
, Theorem 5.6]
. So we omit details.
∎

We then consider the Poisson equations with coefficients:

(2.9)

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} &-k\Delta_{\mathcal{A}}\vartheta=G^{3}\ &\text{in}&\ \Omega,\\
&k\nabla_{\mathcal{A}}\vartheta\cdot\mathcal{N}=G^{6}\ &\text{on}&\ \Sigma,\\
&\vartheta=0\ &\text{on}&\ \Sigma_{s},\end{aligned}\right.

where we assume that
\eta
and
\mathcal{A}
,
\mathcal{N}
, etc. are given.

Theorem 2.3
.

Let
\delta=\pm
. Suppose that
\|\eta\|_{W^{3-1/q_{\delta},q_{\delta}}}<\gamma_{0}
where
\gamma_{0}\ll 1
. Assume that
G^{8}\in L^{q_{\delta}}(\Omega)
and
G^{9}\in W^{1-1/q_{\delta},q_{\delta}}(\Sigma)
. Then there exists a unique
\vartheta\in W^{2,q_{\delta}}(\Omega)
solving (
2.9
). Moreover,

(2.10)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \|\vartheta\|_{W^{2,q_{\delta}}(\Omega)}^{2}\lesssim\|G^{8}\|_{L^{q_{\delta}}(\Omega)}^{2}+\|G^{9}\|_{W^{1-1/q_{\delta},q_{\delta}}(\Sigma)}^{2}.\end{aligned}

Proof.

We rewrite (
2.9
) as the perturbation form:

(2.11)

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} &-k\Delta\vartheta=G^{3}-k\mathop{\rm div}\nolimits_{I-\mathcal{A}}\nabla_{\mathcal{A}}\vartheta-k\mathop{\rm div}\nolimits\nabla_{I-\mathcal{A}}\vartheta\ &\text{in}&\ \Omega,\\
&k\nabla\vartheta\cdot\mathcal{N}_{0}=G^{6}+k\nabla_{I-\mathcal{A}}\vartheta\cdot\mathcal{N}+k\nabla\vartheta\cdot(\mathcal{N}_{0}-\mathcal{N})\ &\text{on}&\ \Sigma,\\
&\vartheta=0\ &\text{on}&\ \Sigma_{s}.\end{aligned}\right.

We now employ fixed point theory to solve (
2.11
). Suppose that
\theta\in W^{2,q_{\delta}}(\Omega)
. Then we define the operator
T_{\eta}:W^{2,q_{\delta}}(\Omega)\to W^{2,q_{\delta}}(\Omega)
via
\theta\mapsto\vartheta=T_{\eta}\theta
, where
\vartheta
and
\theta
satisfy

(2.12)

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} &-k\Delta\vartheta=G^{3}-k\mathop{\rm div}\nolimits_{I-\mathcal{A}}\nabla_{\mathcal{A}}\theta-k\mathop{\rm div}\nolimits\nabla_{I-\mathcal{A}}\theta\ &\text{in}&\ \Omega,\\
&k\nabla\vartheta\cdot\mathcal{N}_{0}=G^{6}+k\nabla_{I-\mathcal{A}}\theta\cdot\mathcal{N}+k\nabla\theta\cdot(\mathcal{N}_{0}-\mathcal{N})\ &\text{on}&\ \Sigma,\\
&\vartheta=0\ &\text{on}&\ \Sigma_{s}.\end{aligned}\right.

In order to use Theorem
2.2
, we need to estimate the right side of (
2.12
). First note that
\nabla\mathcal{A}\sim\nabla^{2}\bar{\eta}
.
We use Hölder inequality, Sobolev embedding and trace theory to estimate

(2.13)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\|\mathop{\rm div}\nolimits_{I-\mathcal{A}}\nabla_{\mathcal{A}}\theta\|_{L^{q_{\delta}}(\Omega)}^{2}+\|\mathop{\rm div}\nolimits\nabla_{I-\mathcal{A}}\theta\|_{L^{q_{\delta}}(\Omega)}^{2}\\
&\lesssim\|I-\mathcal{A}\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}(1+\|\nabla\mathcal{A}\|_{L^{2/(1+\varepsilon_{\delta})}}^{2})\|\nabla\theta\|_{L^{2/(1-\varepsilon_{\delta})}}^{2}+\|I-\mathcal{A}\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}(1+\|\mathcal{A}\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2})\|\theta\|_{W^{2,q_{\delta}}}^{2}\\
&\lesssim\|\eta\|_{W^{3-1/q_{\delta},q_{\delta}}}^{2}\|\theta\|_{W^{2,q_{\delta}}}^{2}.\end{aligned}

Similarly, we use the trace theory to see that

(2.14)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\|\nabla_{I-\mathcal{A}}\theta\cdot\mathcal{N}\|_{W^{1-1/q_{\delta},q_{\delta}}(\Sigma)}^{2}+\|\nabla\theta\cdot(\mathcal{N}_{0}-\mathcal{N})\|_{W^{1-1/q_{\delta},q_{\delta}}(\Sigma)}^{2}\\
&\lesssim\|\nabla_{I-\mathcal{A}}\theta\cdot(\mathcal{N}_{0}-\partial_{1}\bar{\eta}e_{1})\|_{W^{1,q_{\delta}}(\Omega)}^{2}+\|\nabla\theta\cdot\partial_{1}\bar{\eta}e_{1}\|_{W^{1,q_{\delta}}(\Omega)}^{2}\\
&\lesssim\left(\|\nabla\mathcal{A}\|_{L^{2/(1+\varepsilon_{\delta})}}^{2}\|\mathcal{N}_{0}-\partial_{1}\bar{\eta}e_{1}\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}+\|I-\mathcal{A}\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}\|\nabla(\mathcal{N}_{0}-\partial_{1}\bar{\eta}e_{1})\|_{L^{2/(1+\varepsilon_{\delta})}}^{2}\right)\|\nabla\theta\|_{L^{2/(1-\varepsilon_{\delta})}}\\
&\quad+\left(\|I-\mathcal{A}\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}\|\mathcal{N}_{0}-\partial_{1}\bar{\eta}e_{1}\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}+\|\partial_{1}\bar{\eta}\|_{L^{\infty}}^{2}\right)\|\theta\|_{W^{2,q_{\delta}}}^{2}+\|\partial_{1}\bar{\eta}\|_{L^{2/(1+\varepsilon_{\delta})}}^{2}\|\nabla\theta\|_{L^{2/(1-\varepsilon_{\delta})}}\\
&\lesssim\|\eta\|_{W^{3-1/q_{\delta},q_{\delta}}}^{2}\|\theta\|_{W^{2,q_{\delta}}}^{2}.\end{aligned}

With estimates (
2.13
) and (
2.14
), we now use Theorem
2.2
to (
2.12
) to obtain that

\|\vartheta_{1}-\vartheta_{2}\|_{W^{2,q_{\delta}}}^{2}\lesssim\|\eta\|_{W^{3-1/q_{\delta},q_{\delta}}}^{2}\|\theta_{1}-\theta_{2}\|_{W^{2,q_{\delta}}}^{2},

for
T_{\eta}\theta_{j}=\vartheta_{j}
,
j=1,2
.
Then we choose
\gamma_{0}
sufficiently small such that

\|\vartheta_{1}-\vartheta_{2}\|_{W^{2,q_{\delta}}}\leq\frac{1}{2}\|\theta_{1}-\theta_{2}\|_{W^{2,q_{\delta}}},

which yields
T_{\eta}
is strictly contractive.
Thus, we use Banach’s fixed point theory to deduce that (
2.11
) has a unique solution
\vartheta\in W^{2,q_{\delta}}(\Omega)
.
∎

Finally, we consider the equation

(2.15)

\displaystyle\left\{\begin{aligned} &-k\Delta_{\mathcal{A}}\vartheta=G^{8}\ &\text{in}&\ \Omega,\\
&k\nabla_{\mathcal{A}}\vartheta\cdot\mathcal{N}+\vartheta|\mathcal{N}|=G^{9}\ &\text{on}&\ \Sigma,\\
&\vartheta=0\ &\text{on}&\ \Sigma_{s}.\end{aligned}\right.

Theorem 2.4
.

Let
\delta=\pm
. Suppose that
\|\eta\|_{W^{3-1/q_{\delta},q_{\delta}}}<\gamma_{0}
where
\gamma_{0}
is the same as Theorem
2.3
. Assume that
G^{8}\in L^{q_{\delta}}(\Omega)
and
G^{9}\in W^{1-1/q_{\delta},q_{\delta}}(\Sigma)
. Then there exists a unique
\vartheta\in W^{2,q_{\delta}}(\Omega)
solving (
2.15
). Moreover,

(2.16)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \|\vartheta\|_{W^{2,q_{\delta}}(\Omega)}^{2}\lesssim\|G^{8}\|_{L^{q_{\delta}}(\Omega)}^{2}+\|G^{9}\|_{W^{1-1/q_{\delta},q_{\delta}}(\Sigma)}^{2}.\end{aligned}

Proof.

For simplicity, we define the trace operator
R:W^{2,q_{\delta}}(\Omega)\to L^{q_{\delta}}(\Omega)\times W^{1-1/q_{\delta},q_{\delta}}(\Sigma)\times W^{2-1/q_{\delta},q_{\delta}}(\Sigma_{s})
via

R\vartheta=(0,(\vartheta|\mathcal{N}|)|_{\Sigma},0).

Note that
R
is compact, since the embedding
W^{2-1/q_{\delta},q_{\delta}}(\Sigma_{s})\hookrightarrow W^{1-1/q_{\delta},q_{\delta}}(\Sigma_{s})
is compact. So the operator
T_{\eta}+R
is Fredholm, which means the dimensions of kernel and cokernel of
T_{\eta}+R
are both finite. However, for
\vartheta\in\ \text{Ker}(T_{\eta}+R)
, we multiply the first equation in (
2.15
) by
\vartheta J
, and integrate by parts over
\Omega
to see that

(2.17)

\displaystyle k\int_{\Omega}|\nabla_{\mathcal{A}}\vartheta|^{2}J+\int_{\Sigma}|\vartheta|^{2}|\mathcal{N}|=0,

which implies
\vartheta=0
. So
T_{\eta}+R
is injective. Then Fredholm alternative tells us that
T_{\eta}+R
is also surjective. Hence
T_{\eta}+R
is an isomorphism. Thus (
2.15
) is uniquely solvable, i.e.
\vartheta=(T_{\eta}+R)^{-1}(G^{8},G^{9},0)
and the estimate (
2.16
) holds.
∎

3.
Solution to
\mathcal{A}
-linear Heat Equations

In this section, we will establish the well-posedness theory to the
\mathcal{A}
-linear heat equations. We first introduce the definition of weak solutions, then use the Galerkin method to construct weak solutions and strong solutions via elliptic theory. Finally, we use the two–tier energy method to construct solutions in higher regular spaces.

3.1.
Weak Solutions

Definition 3.1
.

Suppose that
\eta
is given as well as
\mathcal{A}
,
J
,
\mathcal{N}
, etc. that are determined in terms of
\eta
as in (
1.9
), (
1.10
). We say that
\theta\in L^{\infty}([0,T];{}_{0}H^{0})\cap L^{2}([0,T];H^{1})
is a weak solution to (
1.19
), provided that

(3.1)

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}\theta,\phi)_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}+k(\nabla_{\mathcal{A}}\theta,\nabla_{\mathcal{A}}\phi)_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}+\int_{\Sigma}\theta\phi|\mathcal{N}|=\int_{\Omega}F^{8}\phi J+\int_{\Sigma}F^{9}\phi

for each
\phi\in{}_{0}H^{1}(\Omega)
, provided that
F^{8}\in L^{q_{+}}(\Omega)
and
F^{9}\in W^{1-1/q_{+},q_{+}}(\Sigma)
.

We now ignore the existence of weak solutions and record the uniqueness on some integral equalities and bounds satisfied by weak solutions, since the existence of weak solutions to (
1.19
) will arise as a byproduct of the construction of strong solutions to (
1.19
).

Lemma 3.1
.

Suppose that
\theta
is a weak solution to (
3.1
). Then for almost every
t\in[0,T]
,

(3.2)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\|\theta(t)\|_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}^{2}+\frac{k}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\|\nabla_{\mathcal{A}}\theta\|_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s+\int_{0}^{t}\|\sqrt{|\mathcal{N}|}\theta\|_{0,\Sigma}^{2}=\frac{1}{2}\|\theta(0)\|_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}^{2}+\int_{\Omega}F^{8}\theta J+\int_{\Sigma}F^{9}\theta.

Moreover,

(3.3)

\displaystyle\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\|\theta(t)\|_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}^{2}+\|\theta\|_{L^{2}H^{0}(\Sigma)}^{2}+\|\nabla_{\mathcal{A}}\theta\|_{\mathcal{H}^{0}_{T}}^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\exp(C_{0}(\eta)T)\Big(\|\theta(0)\|_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}^{2}+\|F^{8}\|_{L^{q_{+}}(\Omega)}^{2}+\|F^{9}\|_{W^{1-1/q_{+},q_{+}}(\Sigma)}^{2}\Big),

where
C_{0}(\eta):=\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\|\partial_{t}JK\|_{L^{\infty}}
.

Proof.

The identity (
3.2
) follows directly from (
3.1
) by using the test function
\phi=\theta\chi_{[0,t]}
, where
\chi
is the characteristic function on
[0,t]
.
From (
3.2
), we can directly derive the inequality by Hölder inequality:

(3.4)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\frac{1}{2}\|\theta(t)\|_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}^{2}+\frac{k}{2}\int_{0}^{t}\|\nabla_{\mathcal{A}}\theta(s)\|_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}^{2}+\|\sqrt{|\mathcal{N}|}\theta(s)\|_{0,\Sigma}^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s\\
&\leq\frac{1}{2}\|\theta(0)\|_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}^{2}+\|J\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}\|F^{8}\|_{L^{q_{+}}(\Omega)}\|\theta\|_{L^{q_{+}/(q_{+}-1)}(\Omega)}\\
&\quad+\|F^{9}\|_{L^{1/(1-\varepsilon_{+})}(\Sigma)}\|\theta\|_{L^{1/\varepsilon_{+}}(\Sigma)}+\frac{1}{2}\|\partial_{t}JK\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\theta(t)\|_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}^{2}.\end{aligned}

The Cauchy inequality and Sobolev embedding inequality then imply that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}\|\theta(t)\|_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}^{2}+\frac{k}{2}\|\nabla_{\mathcal{A}}\theta\|_{\mathcal{H}^{0}_{t}}^{2}+\|\sqrt{|\mathcal{N}|}\theta\|_{L^{2}H^{0}(\Sigma)}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\|\theta(0)\|_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}^{2}+2\|J\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}^{2}\|F^{8}\|_{L^{q_{+}}(\Omega)}^{2}+C\|F^{9}\|_{L^{1/(1-\varepsilon_{+})}(\Sigma)}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\|\partial_{t}JK\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\theta(t)\|_{\mathcal{H}^{0}_{t}}^{2}.

Then Gronwall’s inequality implies (
3.3
).
∎

Proposition 3.2
.

Weak solutions to (
3.1
)
are unique.

Proof.

If
\theta^{1}
and
\theta^{2}
are both weak solutions to (
3.1
), then
\vartheta=\theta^{1}-\theta^{2}
is a weak solution to (
3.1
) with
F^{8}=F^{9}=0
and the initial data
\vartheta(0)=0
. The bound (
3.3
) implies that
\vartheta=0
. Hence, weak solutions to (
3.1
) are unique.
∎

3.2.
Construction of Strong Solutions

Suppose that
\eta
, as well as
\mathcal{A}
,
J
,
\mathcal{N}
, etc. that are determined in terms of
\eta
, are given. We will use Galerkin method to construct strong solutions.

3.2.1.
Galerkin Approximation for Initial Data

In the Galerkin method for
t>0
, we use the tw–tier method to construct solutions: we construct the approximated solutions
\theta^{m}
and
\partial_{t}\theta^{m}
and then pass to the limit to find
\theta
and
\partial_{t}\theta
in the first step, where we need the convergence of initial data
(\theta_{0}^{m},\partial_{t}\theta^{m}(0))
to
(\theta_{0},\partial_{t}\theta(0))
; in the following second step, we use
(\partial_{t}\theta^{m},\theta)
to construct
\partial_{t}^{2}\theta^{m}
, where we need the convergence of initial data
(\partial_{t}\theta^{m}(0),\partial_{t}^{2}\theta^{m}(0))
to
(\partial_{t}\theta(0),\partial_{t}^{2}\theta(0))
. As a preliminary, we can use the following Lemma
3.4
, Propositions
3.5
and
3.8
to prove that

Theorem 3.3
.

There exists a basis
\{w^{k}\}_{k=1}^{\infty}
for the Hilbert space
\mathcal{H}^{1}(t)
for each
t\geq 0
. When
t=0
,
\mathcal{H}^{1}_{m}(0):=span\{w^{1}(0),\ldots,w^{m}(0)\}
, the Galerkin approximated initial data
(\partial_{t}\theta^{m}(0),\partial_{t}^{2}\theta^{m}(0))\in\mathcal{H}^{1}_{m}(0)
obeys the estimates

\displaystyle\|\partial_{t}\theta^{m}(0)\|_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\partial_{t}\theta(0)\|_{H^{0}}+\|\theta_{0}\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma)},

\displaystyle\|\partial_{t}^{2}\theta^{m}(0)\|_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\partial_{t}^{2}\theta(0)\|_{H^{0}}+\|\partial_{t}\theta(0)\|_{H^{1}}+\|\partial_{t}\theta(0)\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma)}.

Moreover,
(\partial_{t}\theta^{m}(0),\partial_{t}^{2}\theta^{m}(0))
converge to
(\partial_{t}\theta(0),\partial_{t}^{2}\theta(0))
weakly in
\mathcal{H}^{0}
.

In order to construct the approximate solutions
\partial_{t}\theta^{m}(t)
for the linear system (
1.19
), we need to construct the initial data
\partial_{t}\theta^{m}(0)
and prove that they are uniformly bounded. Our construction is much subtle due to the orthogonality of basis of
\mathcal{H}^{0}(0)
and
\mathcal{H}^{1}(0)
. For the orthogonality, we define the map
\mathcal{S}:\mathcal{H}^{1}(0)\rightarrow(\mathcal{H}^{1}(0))^{\ast}
via

\left<\mathcal{S}\theta,\phi\right>_{(\mathcal{H}^{1}(0))^{\ast}}=(\theta,\phi)_{\mathcal{H}^{1}(0)},\quad\forall\theta,\phi\in\mathcal{H}^{1}(0)
.
By Riesz representation theorem,
\mathcal{S}
is an isometric isomorphism. We then define
A=\mathcal{S}^{-1}:(\mathcal{H}^{1}(0))^{\ast}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}^{1}(0)
so that for any
f\in(\mathcal{H}^{1}(0))^{\ast}
,
Af\in\mathcal{H}^{1}(0)
is uniquely determined via

\left<f,\phi\right>_{(\mathcal{H}^{1}(0))^{\ast}}=(Af,\phi)_{\mathcal{W}(0)},\quad\forall\phi\in\mathcal{H}^{1}(0)
,
that is equivalent to say that
Af=\theta
is a weak solution to
\mathcal{S}\theta=f
.

Lemma 3.4
.

The operator
A
restricted on
\mathcal{H}^{0}(0)
is a compact, positive and self-adjoint operator. So the eigenvalues of
A
are
\{\lambda_{k}\}_{k=1}^{\infty}
such that
0<\lambda_{1}\leq\lambda_{2}\leq\cdots

and

\lambda_{k}\to\infty
, as
k\to\infty
.
Finally, there exists an orthonormal basis
\{w^{k}\}_{k=1}^{\infty}
of
\mathcal{H}^{0}(0)
, where
w^{k}\in\mathcal{H}^{1}(0)
is an eigenfunction of
\mathcal{S}
corresponding to
\lambda_{k}
:

\mathcal{S}w^{k}=\lambda_{k}w^{k}.

Moreover,
\left\{\frac{w^{k}}{\sqrt{\lambda_{k}}}\right\}_{k=1}^{\infty}
is an orthonormal basis of
\mathcal{H}^{1}(0)
.

Proof.

Note that
\mathcal{H}^{1}(0)\subset\subset\mathcal{H}^{0}(0)\hookrightarrow(\mathcal{H}^{1}(0))^{\ast}
.
We restrict
A|_{\mathcal{H}^{0}(0)}:\mathcal{H}^{0}(0)\rightarrow\mathcal{H}^{1}(0)
and
A|_{\mathcal{H}^{0}(0)}:\mathcal{H}^{0}(0)\rightarrow\mathcal{H}^{0}(0)
is compact.

We claim that
A
is a positive, self-adjoint operator. For
f,g\in\mathcal{H}^{0}(0)
, let
u=Af
and
v=Ag
. Then

(u,v)_{\mathcal{H}^{1}(0)}=(Af,v)_{\mathcal{H}^{1}(0)}=(f,v)_{\mathcal{H}^{0}(0)}=(f,Ag)_{\mathcal{H}^{0}(0)}
,
and

(v,u)_{\mathcal{H}^{1}(0)}=(Ag,u)_{\mathcal{H}^{1}(0)}=(g,u)_{\mathcal{H}^{0}(0)}=(g,Af)_{\mathcal{H}^{0}(0)}
.
By symmetry,
(u,v)_{\mathcal{H}^{1}(0)}=(v,u)_{\mathcal{H}^{1}(0)}
. So

(f,Ag)_{\mathcal{H}^{0}(0)}=(g,Af)_{\mathcal{H}^{0}(0)}=(Af,g)_{\mathcal{H}^{0}(0)}
.
Also,
(Af,f)_{\mathcal{H}^{0}(0)}=(f,f)_{\mathcal{H}^{1}(0)}\geq 0
. Therefore, our claim is valid.

Consequently, by Riesz-Schauder theorem, there exists a complete orthonormal basis
\{w^{k}\}_{k=1}^{\infty}\subseteq\mathcal{H}^{0}(0)
such that

Aw^{k}=\mu_{k}w^{k}
, and
\mu_{k}\to 0
, as
k\to\infty
.
But

Aw^{k}=\mu_{k}w^{k}\in\mathcal{H}^{1}(0)
, for all
w^{k}\in\mathcal{H}^{0}(0)
,
so

\{w^{k}\}_{k=1}^{\infty}\subseteq\mathcal{H}^{1}(0)
,
and

\mu_{k}(w^{k},\psi)_{\mathcal{H}^{1}(0)}=(Aw^{k},\psi)_{\mathcal{H}^{1}(0)}=(w^{k},\psi)_{\mathcal{H}^{0}(0)},\forall\psi\in\mathcal{H}^{1}(0)
.
Then we have

(w^{k},\psi)_{\mathcal{H}^{1}(0)}=\lambda_{k}(w^{k},\psi)_{\mathcal{H}^{0}(0)}
with
\lambda_{k}=\frac{1}{\mu_{k}}\to\infty
, as
k\to\infty
.
Thus
(w^{k},w^{j})_{\mathcal{H}^{1}(0)}=\lambda_{k}(w^{k},w^{j})_{\mathcal{H}^{0}(0)}=\lambda_{k}\delta_{jk}
implies
\{w^{k}/\sqrt{\lambda_{k}}\}
is a complete orthonormal basis of
\mathcal{H}^{1}(0)
.
∎

For the finite dimensional space
\mathcal{H}_{m}^{1}(t)=\text{span}\{w^{1},\ldots,w^{m}\}
with
t\geq 0
, consider the approximate formulation

(3.5)

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}\theta^{m},\psi)_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}+k(\nabla_{\mathcal{A}}\theta^{m},\nabla_{\mathcal{A}}\psi)_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}+(|\mathcal{N}|\theta^{m},\psi)_{0,\Sigma}=\int_{\Omega}F^{8}\psi J+\int_{\Sigma}F^{9}\psi|\mathcal{N}|

for any
\psi\in\mathcal{H}_{m}^{1}(t)
. For our purpose to construct
\partial_{t}\theta^{m}(t)
, we define

\theta^{m}(t)=\sum_{j=1}^{m}d_{j}(t)w^{j}
, so that
\partial_{t}\theta^{m}(t)=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\dot{d}_{j}(t)w^{j}
.

By choose
t=0
in (
3.5
), we have

(3.6)

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}\theta^{m}(0),\psi)_{\mathcal{H}^{0}(0)}+k(\nabla_{\mathcal{A}(0)}\theta^{m}(0),\nabla_{\mathcal{A}(0)}\psi)_{\mathcal{H}^{0}(0)}+(|\mathcal{N}(0)|\theta^{m}(0),\psi)_{0,\Sigma}

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}F^{8}(0)\psi J(0)+\int_{\Sigma}F^{9}(0)\psi|\mathcal{N}(0)|

for each
\psi\in\mathcal{H}_{m}^{1}(0)
. Here
\theta^{m}_{0}
is the projection of
\theta_{0}
onto
\mathcal{H}_{m}^{1}(0)
. Then we take
\psi=w^{j}
so that

\displaystyle A_{kj}\dot{d}_{j}(0)+B_{k,j}d_{j}(0)=\int_{\Omega}F^{8}(0)w^{k}\psi J(0)+\int_{\Sigma}F^{9}(0)w^{k}|\mathcal{N}(0)|,

where
A_{kj}=(w^{k},w^{j})_{\mathcal{H}^{0}(0)}
,
B_{k,j}=k(\nabla_{\mathcal{A}(0)}w^{k},\nabla_{\mathcal{A}(0)}w^{j})_{\mathcal{H}^{0}(0)}+(|\mathcal{N}(0)|w^{k},w^{j})_{0,\Sigma}
. Due to the Lemma
3.4
, the matrix
A=(A_{kj})
is invertible. So we get the formula of
\dot{d}_{j}(0)
and
\partial_{t}\theta^{m}(0)
.

Proposition 3.5
.

The sequences
\{\partial_{t}\theta^{m}(0)\}
constructed in (
3.6
) are uniformly bounded such that

(3.7)

\|\partial_{t}\theta^{m}(0)\|_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}\lesssim\|\partial_{t}\theta(0)\|_{H^{0}}+\|\theta_{0}\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma)}

uniformly for
\varepsilon\leq 1
.

Proof.

From (
3.2
),

(3.8)

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}\theta(0),\psi)_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}+k(\nabla_{\mathcal{A}(0)}\theta_{0},\nabla_{\mathcal{A}(0)}\psi)_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}+(\theta_{0}|\mathcal{N}(0)|,\psi)_{0,\Sigma}

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}F^{8}(0)\psi J(0)+\int_{\Sigma}F^{9}(0)\psi|\mathcal{N}(0)|,

for each
\psi\in\mathcal{H}_{m}^{1}(0)
. Since
\left\{\frac{w^{k}}{\sqrt{\lambda_{k}}}\right\}_{k=1}^{\infty}
is a complete orthonormal basis of
\mathcal{H}^{1}(0)
, we can represent
\theta_{0}
and also
\theta_{0}^{m}
by

\theta_{0}=\sum_{j=0}^{\infty}a_{j}\frac{w^{j}}{\sqrt{\lambda_{j}}}
, and
\theta_{0}^{m}=\sum_{j=0}^{m}a_{j}\frac{w^{j}}{\sqrt{\lambda_{j}}}

for some
a_{j}\in\mathbb{R}
.
For each
\psi=w^{j}/\sqrt{\lambda_{j}}
,
j=1,\ldots,m
, we use the orthogonality of basis
\{w^{k}/\sqrt{\lambda_{k}}\}
to arrive that

\left(\theta_{0},\frac{w^{j}}{\sqrt{\lambda_{j}}}\right)_{\mathcal{H}^{1}}=a_{j}=\left(\theta_{0}^{m},\frac{w^{j}}{\sqrt{\lambda_{j}}}\right)_{\mathcal{H}^{1}}

and hence
(\theta_{0},\psi)_{\mathcal{H}^{1}}=(\theta_{0}^{m},\psi)_{\mathcal{H}^{1}}

for any
\psi\in\mathcal{H}^{1}_{m}(0)
by linear superposition.
So the subtraction between (
3.6
) and (
3.8
) gives us that

(3.9)

(\partial_{t}\theta^{m}(0)-\partial_{t}\theta(0),\psi)_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}=0.

By taking
\psi=\partial_{t}\theta^{m}(0)
in (
3.9
), we use the Cauchy’s inequality to deduce that

(3.10)

\|\partial_{t}\theta^{m}(0)\|_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}^{2}\lesssim\|\partial_{t}\theta(0)\|_{H^{0}}\|\partial_{t}\theta^{m}(0)\|_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}

implying (
3.7
).
∎

So we can show that

Proposition 3.6
.

It holds that
\partial_{t}\theta^{m}(0)
converges to
\partial_{t}\theta(0)
weakly in
\mathcal{H}^{0}
as
m\to\infty
.

Proof.

The uniform boundedness for
\partial_{t}\theta^{m}(0)
in (
3.7
) allows us to take a weak limit in
\mathcal{H}^{0}
, up to an extraction of a subsequence,

\partial_{t}\theta^{m}(0)\rightharpoonup w\quad\text{in}\ \mathcal{H}^{0}
,
for some
w\in{}_{0}H^{0}
.
By passing to the limit
m\to\infty
for (
3.9
),
we have

(3.11)

(w-\partial_{t}\theta(0),\psi)_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}=0

for any
\psi\in\mathcal{H}^{1}(0)
. We now switch the domain
\Omega
to the initial state of original deformable domain
\Omega(0)
by the inverse mapping
\Phi(0)^{-1}
and let
\tilde{w}\circ\Phi(0)=w,\ \widetilde{\partial_{t}\theta}(0)\circ\Phi(0)=\partial_{t}\theta(0)
,
so that
\tilde{w}\in L^{2}(\Omega(0))
and

\widetilde{\partial_{t}\theta}(0)\in H^{1}(\Omega(0))\simeq\{\theta\in L^{2}(\Omega(0)):\|\nabla\theta\|_{L^{2}(\Omega(0))}^{2}+\|\theta|\mathcal{N}(0)|\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma)}^{2}<\infty\}
.
We choose
\psi
in the closure of
C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)
under
H^{1}
norm, so that (
3.11
) is reduce to
(\tilde{w}-\widetilde{\partial_{t}\theta}(0),\psi)_{L^{2}(\Omega(0))}=0
.
Since
C_{c}^{\infty}(\Omega)
is dense in
L^{2}(\Omega)
, we have that

\tilde{w}-\widetilde{\partial_{t}\theta}(0)=0
implying
w=\partial_{t}\theta(0)
by
\Phi(0)^{-1}
.
∎

Based on the weak formula of

(3.12)

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}^{2}\theta,\psi)_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}+k(\nabla_{\mathcal{A}}\partial_{t}\theta,\nabla_{\mathcal{A}}\psi)_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}+(\partial_{t}\theta,\psi|\mathcal{N}|)_{L^{2}(\Sigma)}=\left<\mathscr{F}_{2},\psi\right>

for any
\psi\in\mathcal{H}^{1}
, where

\displaystyle\left<\mathscr{F}_{2},\psi\right>

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}\partial_{t}F^{8}\psi J+(F^{8}-\partial_{t}\theta\psi\partial_{t}J)-k\nabla_{\partial_{t}\mathcal{A}}\theta\cdot\nabla_{\mathcal{A}}\psi J+\nabla_{\mathcal{A}}\theta\cdot\nabla_{\partial_{t}\mathcal{A}}\psi J+\nabla_{\mathcal{A}}\theta\cdot\nabla_{\mathcal{A}}\psi\partial_{t}J

\displaystyle+\int_{\Sigma}\partial_{t}F^{9}\psi-k\partial_{t}\mathcal{N}\cdot\nabla_{\mathcal{A}}\nabla\theta+k\mathcal{N}\cdot\nabla_{\partial_{t}\mathcal{A}}\nabla\theta-\theta\partial_{t}|\mathcal{N}|,

we apply the two–tier method of Galerkin approximation to construct
\partial_{t}\theta(0)
. We assume that
(\partial_{t}\theta)^{m}:=\sum_{j=1}^{m}e_{j}w^{j}
, where
e_{j}
is the coefficient. Then we construct
\partial_{t}(\partial_{t}\theta)^{m}=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\dot{e}_{j}w^{j}
,
t\geq 0
and the initial data
\partial_{t}(\partial_{t}\theta)^{m}(0)=\sum_{j=1}^{m}\dot{e}_{j}(0)w^{j}\in\mathcal{H}^{1}_{m}(0)
. When
t=0
,
\partial_{t}(\partial_{t}\theta)^{m}(0)
is the projection of
\partial_{t}^{2}\theta(0)
onto
\mathcal{H}^{1}_{m}(0)
and
\partial_{t}(\partial_{t}\theta)^{m}(0)
is supposed to satisfying

(3.13)

(\partial_{t}(\partial_{t}\theta)^{m}(0),\psi)_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}+k(\nabla_{\mathcal{A}(0)}(\partial_{t}\theta)^{m}(0),\nabla_{\mathcal{A}(0)}\psi)_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}+((\partial_{t}\theta)^{m}(0),\psi|\mathcal{N}(0)|)_{L^{2}(\Sigma)}=\mathscr{F}_{2}(\psi),

then we take
\psi=w^{j}(0)
so that (
3.13
) is reduced to

\displaystyle\dot{e}_{j}(0)+\lambda_{j}e_{j}(0)=\mathscr{F}_{2}(w^{j}).

Consequently, we have the formula of
\partial_{t}(\partial_{t}\theta)^{m}(0)
.

We now show the uniform bound and convergence for
\partial_{t}(\partial_{t}\theta)^{m}(0)
.

Proposition 3.7
.

(3.14)

\displaystyle\|\partial_{t}(\partial_{t}\theta)^{m}(0)\|_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}\lesssim\|\partial_{t}(\partial_{t}\theta)(0)\|_{H^{0}}+\|\partial_{t}\theta(0)\|_{H^{1}}+\|\partial_{t}\theta(0)\|_{L^{2}(\Sigma)}.

Proof.

By orthogonality of the basis
\{w^{k}/\sqrt{\lambda_{k}}\}
, we have

\displaystyle((\partial_{t}\theta)^{m}(0),\psi)_{\mathcal{H}^{1}}

\displaystyle=k(\nabla_{\mathcal{A}(0)}(\partial_{t}\theta)^{m}(0),\psi))+((\partial_{t}\theta)^{m}(0),\psi|\mathcal{N}(0)|)_{L^{2}(\Sigma)}

\displaystyle=k(\nabla_{\mathcal{A}(0)}(\partial_{t}\theta)(0),\psi))+((\partial_{t}\theta)(0),\psi|\mathcal{N}(0)|)_{L^{2}(\Sigma)}

\displaystyle=((\partial_{t}\theta)(0),\psi)_{\mathcal{H}^{1}}

for each
\psi\in\mathcal{H}^{1}_{m}(0)
. So the difference between (
3.13
) and (
3.12
) at
t=0
gives us that

(3.15)

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}(\partial_{t}\theta)^{m}(0)-\partial_{t}^{2}\theta(0),\psi)_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}=0.

After taking
\psi=(\partial_{t}\theta)^{m}(0)
in (
3.15
), we use the Cauchy’s inequality to deduce that

(3.16)

\displaystyle\|\partial_{t}(\partial_{t}\theta)^{m}(0)\|_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}\lesssim\|\partial_{t}^{2}\theta(0)\|_{H^{0}}

implying (
3.14
)
uniformly for
m
.
∎

Proposition 3.8
.

\partial_{t}(\partial_{t}\theta)^{m}(0)
converges to
\partial_{t}^{2}\theta(0)
weakly in
\mathcal{H}^{0}_{\sigma}
as
m\to\infty
.

Proof.

The uniform boundedness in Proposition
3.7
for
(\partial_{t}\theta)^{m}
allows us to take a weak limit in
H^{0}
. Using the argument as in the proof of Proposition
3.6
, we have that up to an extraction of a subsequence,
\partial(\partial_{t}\theta)^{m}(0)\rightharpoonup\partial_{t}^{2}\theta)(0)
in
H^{0}
.

By passing the limit
m\to\infty
for (
3.13
), we can obtain

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}^{2}\theta(0),\psi)_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}+k(\nabla_{\mathcal{A}(0)}(\partial_{t}\theta)(0),\nabla_{\mathcal{A}(0)}\psi)_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}+((\partial_{t}\theta)(0),\psi|\mathcal{N}(0)|)_{L^{2}(\Sigma)}=\mathscr{F}_{2}(\psi),

for each
\psi\in\mathcal{H}^{1}(0)
.
∎

Remark 3.1
.

The convergence of
\partial_{t}^{2}\theta^{m}(0)
to
\partial_{t}^{2}\theta(0)
in
H^{0}(\Omega)
is actually used to prove Theorem
3.12
in the step of applying Theorem
3.9
, since the convergence
\partial_{t}(\partial_{t}\theta)^{m}(0)\to\partial_{t}(\partial_{t}\theta)(0)
in
H^{0}
is necessary. More precisely, following the procedure of proving Theorem
3.9
, we need to construct the approximate solution
(\partial_{t}\theta)^{m}
, and then construct
\partial_{t}(\partial_{t}\theta)^{m}
based on
\partial_{t}(\partial_{t}\theta)^{m}(0)
.

3.3.
Construction of Strong Solutions to (
1.19
)

For some
T>0
, we define

\displaystyle\mathfrak{D}(\eta):=\|\eta\|_{L^{2}W^{3-1/q_{+},q_{+}}}^{2}+\|\partial_{t}\eta\|_{L^{2}W^{3-1/q_{-},q_{-}}}^{2}+\sum_{j=0}^{2}\|\partial_{t}^{j}\eta\|_{L^{2}H^{3/2-\alpha}}^{2}+\|\partial_{t}^{3}\eta\|_{L^{2}H^{1/2-\alpha}}^{2},

\displaystyle\mathfrak{E}(\eta):=\|\eta\|_{L^{\infty}W^{3-1/q_{+},q_{+}}}^{2}+\|\partial_{t}\eta\|_{L^{\infty}H^{3/2+(\varepsilon_{-}-\alpha)/2}}^{2}+\sum_{j=0}^{2}\|\partial_{t}^{j}\eta\|_{L^{\infty}H^{1}}^{2},\quad\mathfrak{K}(\eta):=\mathfrak{D}(\eta)+\mathfrak{E}(\eta).

We say
\theta\in L^{\infty}W^{2,q_{+}}
is a strong solution to (
1.20
), provided that
\partial_{t}^{k}\theta\in L^{\infty}H^{0}\cap L^{2}H^{1}
,
k=0,1
, such that
\theta
satisfies (
1.19
) when
j=0
.

In the following theorem, we give the construction of strong solutions to (
1.19
) by Galerkin method. The strategy consists four main steps. The first is to use the basis for
\mathcal{H}^{1}(t)
and ODE theory to construct
\theta^{m}
in a finite dimensional space with dimension
m
. Secondly, we use energy estimate to find a uniform bound for
\theta^{m}
; we then take time derivative to find the formulation of
\partial_{t}\theta^{m}
and use the energy estimates to find the uniform bound for
\partial_{t}\theta^{m}
. Thirdly, we can pass to the limit
m\to\infty
to get the weak formulation, and use Theorem
2.4
to obtain the strong solution. Lastly, we construct the weak solution
\partial_{t}\theta
to (
1.19
) when
j=1
.

Theorem 3.9
.

Assume the initial data are constructed in Section
B.1
.
Suppose that
\mathfrak{K}(\eta)\leq\delta
is smaller than
\delta_{0}
in Lemma
1.3
and Theorem 4.7 in
[
11
]
.
Then there exists a unique strong solution
\theta
solving (
1.19
). Moreover, the solution obeys the estimate

(3.17)

\displaystyle\sum_{j=0}^{1}\|\partial_{t}^{j}\theta\|_{L^{\infty}H^{0}}^{2}+\|\theta\|_{L^{2}W^{2,q_{+}}}^{2}+\sum_{j=0}^{1}\left(\|\partial_{t}^{j}\theta\|_{L^{2}H^{1}}^{2}+\|\partial_{t}^{j}\theta\|_{L^{2}H^{0}(\Sigma)}^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\lesssim\exp\{\mathfrak{E}(\eta)T\}\bigg(\mathcal{E}(0)+\|(F^{8}+F^{9})(0)\|_{(H^{1})^{\ast}}^{2}+(1+\mathfrak{E}(\eta))(\|F^{8}\|_{L^{2}L^{q_{+}}}^{2}

\displaystyle+\|F^{9}\|_{L^{2}W^{1-1/q_{+},q_{+}}}^{2})+\|\partial_{t}(F^{8}+F^{9})\|_{(\mathcal{H}^{1}_{T})^{\ast}}^{2}\bigg),

where ”
\lesssim
” omits a constant depending on
(g,\sigma,\ell,|\Omega|)
.
Moreover,
\partial_{t}\theta
satisfies

(3.18)

\left\{\begin{aligned} &\partial_{t}^{2}\theta-k\Delta_{\mathcal{A}}\partial_{t}\theta=\partial_{t}F^{8}+G^{8}\quad&\text{in}&\quad\Omega,\\
&k\mathcal{N}\cdot\nabla_{\mathcal{A}}\partial_{t}\theta+\partial_{t}\theta|\mathcal{N}|=\partial_{t}F^{9}+G^{9}\quad&\text{on}&\quad\Sigma,\\
&\partial_{t}\theta=0\quad&\text{on}&\quad\Sigma_{s},\end{aligned}\right.

in the pressureless weak sense of (
3.1
), where
G^{8}
and
G^{9}
are defined by

\displaystyle G^{8}

\displaystyle=k\mathop{\rm div}\nolimits_{\mathcal{A}}\nabla_{\partial_{t}\mathcal{A}}\theta+k\mathop{\rm div}\nolimits_{\partial_{t}\mathcal{A}}\nabla_{\mathcal{A}}\theta,

\displaystyle G^{9}

\displaystyle=-k\nabla_{\partial_{t}\mathcal{A}}\theta\cdot\mathcal{N}-k\nabla_{\mathcal{A}}\theta\cdot\partial_{t}\mathcal{N}-\theta\partial_{t}|\mathcal{N}|.

More precisely, (
3.18
) holds in the weak sense of

(3.19)

\displaystyle\left<\partial_{t}^{2}\theta,\psi\right>_{\ast}+k\int_{0}^{T}(\partial_{t}\theta,\psi)_{\mathcal{H}^{1}}

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}(\partial_{t}F^{8}+G^{8})\cdot\psi J+\int_{0}^{T}\int_{-\ell}^{\ell}(\partial_{t}F^{9}+G^{9})\psi

for each
\psi\in\mathcal{H}^{1}_{T}
.

Proof.

Step 1 – Galerkin Setup.
In order to utilize the Galerkin method, we choose the same basis
\{w^{j}(t)\}_{j=1}^{\infty}
of
\mathcal{H}^{1}
constructed in Lemma
3.4
.
For any integer
m\geq 1
, we define the finite dimensional space

\mathcal{H}^{1}_{m}:=\text{span}\{w^{1},\cdots,w^{m}\}\subseteq\mathcal{H}^{1}
,
and we write

\mathcal{P}^{m}:\mathcal{H}^{1}\rightarrow\mathcal{H}^{1}_{m}

for the
\mathcal{H}^{1}
orthogonal projection onto
\mathcal{H}^{1}_{m}
. Clearly, for each
w\in\mathcal{H}^{1}
, we have that
\mathcal{P}^{m}w\rightarrow w
as
m\rightarrow\infty
.

Step 2 – Solving the Approximate Problem.
For each
m\geq 1
, we define an approximate solution
\theta^{m}(t):=d^{m}_{j}(t)w^{j}
, with
d^{m}_{j}:[0,T]\rightarrow\mathbb{R}\ \text{for}\ j=1,\dots,m
,
where we used the Einstein convention of summation of the repeated index
j
.

We want to choose the coefficients
d^{m}_{j}(t)
so that

(3.20)

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}\theta^{m},\varphi)_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}+k(\nabla_{\mathcal{A}}\theta^{m},\nabla_{\mathcal{A}}\varphi)_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}+(\theta^{m},\varphi|\mathcal{N}|)_{0,\Sigma}=\int_{\Omega}F^{8}\cdot\varphi J+\int_{\Sigma}F^{9}\varphi,

for each
\varphi\in\mathcal{H}^{1}_{m}(t)
. We supplement the initial data

\theta^{m}(0)=\mathcal{P}^{m}_{0}\theta_{0}\in\mathcal{H}^{1}_{m}(0)
.

Then we see that (
3.20
) is equivalent to the equation of
d^{m}_{j}
given by

(3.21)

\displaystyle\dot{d}^{m}_{i}(w^{i},w^{j})_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}+kd^{m}_{i}\left((\nabla_{\mathcal{A}}w^{i},\nabla_{\mathcal{A}}w^{j})_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}+(w^{i},w^{j}|\mathcal{N}|)_{0,\Sigma}\right)=\int_{\Omega}F^{8}\cdot w^{j}J+\int_{\Sigma}F^{9}w^{j},

for
i,j=1,\dots,m
. Since
\{w^{j}\}_{j=1}^{\infty}
is a basis of
\mathcal{H}^{1}(t)
, the
m\times m
matrix with
j,k
entry
(w^{i},w^{j})_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}
is invertible. Thus the initial data
d^{m}_{i}(0)
is determined uniquely via

d^{m}_{i}(0)(w^{i},w^{j})_{\mathcal{H}^{1}}=(\mathcal{P}_{0}^{m}\theta_{0},w^{j})_{\mathcal{H}^{1}}.

Then we view (
3.21
) as an differential system of the form

(3.22)

\dot{d}^{m}(t)+\mathfrak{B}(t)d^{m}(t)=\mathfrak{F}(t).

The
m\times m
matrix
\mathfrak{B}
belongs to
C^{1}([0,T])
and the forcing term
\mathfrak{F}\in C^{1}([0,T])
. By the standard linear theory of ODE, (
3.21
) has a unique solution
d^{m}\in C^{1,1}([0,T])
achieving the initial data
d^{m}(0)
.

Step 3 – Energy Estimates for
\theta^{m}
.
By construction,
\theta^{m}(t)\in\mathcal{H}^{1}_{m}(t)
, so we may choose
w=\theta^{m}
as a test function in (
3.20
) such that we have

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\frac{1}{2}\|\theta^{m}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}^{2}+\|\nabla_{\mathcal{A}}\theta^{m}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}^{2}+\int_{\Sigma}|\theta^{m}|^{2}|\mathcal{N}|=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Omega}\partial_{t}JK|\theta^{m}|^{2}J+\int_{\Omega}F^{8}\cdot\theta^{m}J+\int_{\Sigma}F^{9}\theta^{m}.

Then using the Hölder inequality with
\frac{1}{q_{+}}+\frac{2}{\varepsilon_{+}}=1
, and the Cauchy inequality, we have that

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\frac{1}{2}\|\theta^{m}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}^{2}+k\|\nabla_{\mathcal{A}}\theta^{m}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}^{2}+\|\theta^{m}\|_{H^{0}(\Sigma)}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\|\partial_{t}JK\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\theta^{m}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}^{2}+\|J\|_{L^{\infty}(\Sigma)}\|F^{8}\|_{L^{q_{+}}}\|\theta^{m}\|_{L^{2/\varepsilon_{+}}}+\|F^{9}\|_{L^{q_{+}}(\Sigma)}\|\theta^{m}\|_{L^{2/\varepsilon_{+}}(\Sigma)}.

Then we employ the Cauchy’s inequality, Sobolev embedding theory
H^{1}(U)\hookrightarrow L^{2/\varepsilon_{+}}(U)
for
U=\Omega,\Sigma
, and the usual trace theory to derive that

(3.23)

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\frac{1}{2}\|\theta^{m}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}(\|\theta^{m}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}}^{2}+\|\theta^{m}\|_{H^{0}(\Sigma)})

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\|\partial_{t}JK\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\theta^{m}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}^{2}+C(1+\|\eta\|_{W^{3-1/q_{+},q_{+}}}^{2})(\|F^{8}\|_{L^{q_{+}}}^{2}+\|F^{9}\|_{L^{q_{+}}}^{2}).

Since
\mathcal{P}_{0}
is projected orthogonal, then

\|\theta^{m}(0)\|_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}\lesssim\|\theta^{m}(0)\|_{H^{0}}\lesssim\|\theta_{0}\|_{1}\lesssim\|\theta_{0}\|_{W^{2,q_{+}}}.

Then the Gronwall’s lemma and Lemma
1.3
reveal that

(3.24)

\displaystyle\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\|\theta^{m}\|_{H^{0}}^{2}+\|\theta^{m}\|_{L^{2}H^{1}(\Omega)}^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\exp(C_{0}(\eta))\bigg(\|\theta_{0}\|_{W^{2,q_{+}}}^{2}+(1+\|\eta\|_{L^{\infty}W^{3-1/q_{+},q_{+}}}^{2})\Big(\|F^{8}\|_{L^{2}L^{q_{+}}}^{2}+\|F^{9}\|_{L^{2}L^{q_{+}}}^{2}\Big)\bigg),

where
C_{0}(\eta)=\int_{0}^{T}\|\partial_{t}JK\|_{L^{\infty}}\lesssim T\|\partial_{t}\eta\|_{L^{\infty}H^{3/2+(\varepsilon_{-}-\alpha)/2}}
.

Step 4 – Energy Estimate for
\partial_{t}\theta^{m}
.
Suppose that
w=b_{j}^{m}w^{j}\in\mathcal{H}^{1}_{m}(t)
for
b_{j}^{m}\in C^{1,1}([0,T])
. We now use this
w
in (
3.20
), temporally differentiate the resulting equation, and then subtract this from the equation (
3.20
) with test function
\partial_{t}w
. This eliminates the terms of
\partial_{t}w
and leaves us with the equality

(3.25)

\displaystyle\left(\partial_{t}^{2}\theta^{m},w\right)_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}+k(\nabla_{\mathcal{A}}\partial_{t}\theta^{m},\nabla_{\mathcal{A}}w)_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}+\int_{\Sigma}\partial_{t}\theta^{m}w|\mathcal{N}|

\displaystyle=-(\partial_{t}\theta^{m},w\partial_{t}JK)_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}-\int_{\Sigma}\theta^{m}w\partial_{t}|\mathcal{N}|

\displaystyle-k\int_{\Omega}(\nabla_{\partial_{t}\mathcal{A}}\theta^{m}\cdot\nabla_{\mathcal{A}}w+\nabla_{\mathcal{A}}\theta^{m}\cdot\nabla_{\partial_{t}\mathcal{A}}w+\partial_{t}JK\nabla_{\mathcal{A}}\theta^{m}\cdot\nabla_{\mathcal{A}}w)J

\displaystyle+\int_{\Omega}\partial_{t}F^{8}\cdot wJ+\int_{\Sigma}\partial_{t}F^{9}w+\int_{\Omega}F^{8}\cdot w\partial_{t}J.

By plugging the test function
w=\partial_{t}\theta^{m}
into (
3.25
),
we have

(3.26)

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\frac{1}{2}\|\partial_{t}\theta^{m}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}^{2}+k\|\nabla_{\mathcal{A}}\partial_{t}\theta^{m}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}^{2}+\int_{\Sigma}|\partial_{t}\theta^{m}||\mathcal{N}|=I+II+III,

where

I=-\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Omega}|\partial_{t}\theta^{m}|^{2}\partial_{t}J-\int_{\Sigma}\theta^{m}\partial_{t}\theta^{m}\partial_{t}|\mathcal{N}|,

\displaystyle II=-k\int_{\Omega}(\nabla_{\partial_{t}\mathcal{A}}\theta^{m}\cdot\nabla_{\mathcal{A}}\partial_{t}\theta^{m}+\nabla_{\mathcal{A}}\theta^{m}\cdot\nabla_{\partial_{t}\mathcal{A}}\partial_{t}\theta^{m}+\partial_{t}JK\nabla_{\mathcal{A}}\theta^{m}\cdot\nabla_{\mathcal{A}}\partial_{t}\theta^{m})J,

\displaystyle III=\int_{\Omega}\partial_{t}F^{8}\cdot\partial_{t}\theta^{m}J+\int_{\Sigma}\partial_{t}F^{9}\partial_{t}\theta^{m}|\mathcal{N}|+\int_{\Omega}F^{8}\cdot\partial_{t}\theta^{m}\partial_{t}J.

We now estimate each term of right-hand side in (
3.26
). We first employ the Hölder inequality and Sobolev embedding theory to derive that

(3.27)

\displaystyle I\leq C(\|\partial_{t}JK\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)})\|\partial_{t}\theta^{m}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}^{2}+C(\|\partial_{t}J\|_{L^{\infty}(\partial\Omega)})\|\theta^{m}\|_{H^{0}(\Sigma)}\|\partial_{t}\theta^{m}\|_{H^{0}(\Sigma)}

\displaystyle\leq C(\|\partial_{t}\eta\|_{H^{3/2+(\varepsilon_{-}-\alpha)/2}}^{2})(\|\partial_{t}\theta^{m}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}^{2}+\|\theta^{m}\|_{H^{0}(\Sigma)}^{2})+\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Sigma}|\partial_{t}\theta^{m}|^{2}J.

Similarly, we estimate

(3.28)

\displaystyle II

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\partial_{t}\mathcal{A}\|_{L^{\infty}}\|J\|_{L^{\infty}}(\|\theta^{m}\|_{\dot{H}^{1}}\|\nabla_{\mathcal{A}}\partial_{t}\theta^{m}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}+\|\partial_{t}\theta^{m}\|_{\dot{H}^{1}}\|\nabla_{\mathcal{A}}\theta^{m}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{0}})

\displaystyle+\|\partial_{t}JK\|_{L^{\infty}}\|\nabla_{\mathcal{A}}\partial_{t}\theta^{m}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}\|\nabla_{\mathcal{A}}\theta^{m}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}

\displaystyle\leq C(\|\partial_{t}\eta\|_{H^{3/2+(\varepsilon_{-}-\alpha)/2}}^{2})\|\nabla_{\mathcal{A}}\theta^{m}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\|\nabla_{\mathcal{A}}\partial_{t}\theta^{m}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}^{2}.

We now turn to
III
. We first use the dual space estimate to bound terms related to one time derivative of forces:

(3.29)

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\partial_{t}F^{8}\cdot(\partial_{t}\theta^{m})J+\int_{\Sigma}\partial_{t}F^{9}\cdot(\partial_{t}\theta^{m})\lesssim\|\partial_{t}(F^{8}+F^{9})\|_{(\mathcal{H}^{1})^{\ast}}\|\partial_{t}\theta^{m}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}}.

For terms with
F^{8}
, we estimate it by Hölder inequality with
\frac{1}{q_{+}}+\frac{\varepsilon_{+}}{2}=1
and Sobolev embedding with
H^{1}\hookrightarrow L^{\varepsilon_{+}/2}
:

(3.30)

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\left[\partial_{t}JF^{8}(\partial_{t}\theta^{m})\right]

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\partial_{t}J\|_{L^{\infty}}\|F^{8}\|_{L^{q_{+}}(\Omega)}\|\partial_{t}\theta^{m}\|_{L^{\varepsilon_{+}/2}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle\lesssim\|\partial_{t}\eta\|_{H^{3/2+(\varepsilon_{-}-\alpha)/2}}\|F^{8}\|_{L^{q_{+}}(\Omega)}\|\partial_{t}\theta^{m}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}}.

Thus, by the Schwarz inequality and combining (
3.27
)–(
3.30
), we have the energy structure

(3.31)

\displaystyle\frac{d}{dt}\frac{1}{2}\|\partial_{t}\theta^{m}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}^{2}+\frac{1}{4}\|\partial_{t}\theta^{m}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C\|\partial_{t}\eta\|_{H^{3/2+(\varepsilon_{-}-\alpha)/2}}\left(\frac{1}{2}\|\partial_{t}\theta^{m}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}^{2}\right)+C\|\partial_{t}\eta\|_{H^{3/2+(\varepsilon_{-}-\alpha)/2}}^{2}(\|\theta^{m}\|_{\mathcal{H}^{1}}^{2}+\|\theta^{m}\|_{H^{0}(\Sigma)}^{2})

\displaystyle+C\|\partial_{t}(F^{8}+F^{9})\|_{(\mathcal{H}^{1})^{\ast}}^{2}+C\|\partial_{t}\eta\|_{H^{3/2+(\varepsilon_{-}-\alpha)/2}}^{2}\|F^{8}\|_{L^{q_{+}}}^{2}.

Note that the derivative
\{\|\partial_{t}\theta^{m}(0)\|_{H^{0}}\}
are uniformly bounded , as proved in Theorem
3.3
. We then employ the Gronwall’s inequality, Hölder inequality, the smallness of
\mathfrak{K}(\eta)
and the initial data
\partial_{t}\theta(0)
to derive that

(3.32)

\displaystyle\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\|\partial_{t}\theta^{m}\|_{0}^{2}+\|\partial_{t}\theta^{m}\|_{L^{2}H^{1}}^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\exp\{\|\partial_{t}\eta\|_{L^{\infty}H^{3/2+\varepsilon_{-}/2}}T\}\bigg(\|\partial_{t}\theta(0)\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}^{2}+\|(F^{8}+F^{9})(0)\|_{(H^{1})^{\ast}}^{2}

\displaystyle+\|\partial_{t}\eta\|_{L^{\infty}H^{3/2+\varepsilon_{-}/2}}^{2}(\|F^{8}\|_{L^{2}L^{q_{+}}}^{2}+\|F^{9}\|_{L^{2}W^{1-1/q_{+},q_{+}}}^{2}+\|\partial_{t}(F^{8}+F^{9})\|_{(\mathcal{H}^{1}_{T})^{\ast}}^{2})\bigg).

Step 5 – Passing to the Limit.
We now utilize the energy estimates (
3.24
) and (
3.32
) to pass to the limit
m\rightarrow\infty
. According to Lemma
1.3
and energy estimates, we have that the sequence
\{\theta^{m}\}
and
\{\partial_{t}\theta^{m}\}
are uniformly bounded both in
L^{2}{}_{0}H^{1}
, and
L^{\infty}H^{0}
. Up to the extraction of a subsequence, we then know that

(3.33)

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \theta^{m}\rightharpoonup\theta\ \text{weakly-}\ \text{in}\ L^{2}{}_{0}H^{1},\quad\partial_{t}\theta^{m}\rightharpoonup\partial_{t}\theta\ \text{weakly in}\ L^{2}{}_{0}H^{1},\\
\theta^{m}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\rightharpoonup}}\theta\ \text{weakly-}\ast\ \text{in}\ L^{\infty}H^{0},\quad\partial_{t}\theta^{m}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\ast}}{{\rightharpoonup}}\partial_{t}\theta\ \text{weakly-}\ast\ \text{in}\ L^{\infty}H^{0}.\end{aligned}

By lower semicontinuity, the energy estimates imply that

(3.34)

\displaystyle\|\theta\|_{L^{\infty}H^{0}}^{2}+\|\partial_{t}\theta\|_{L^{\infty}H^{0}}^{2}+\|\theta\|_{L^{2}H^{1}}^{2}+\|\partial_{t}\theta\|_{L^{2}H^{1}}^{2}

is bounded by (
3.24
) and (
3.32
).

Step 6 – Strong Solutions.
Due to the convergence, we may pass to the limit in (
3.20
) for almost every
t\in[0,T]
so that

(3.35)

\displaystyle(\partial_{t}\theta,w)_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}+k(\nabla_{\mathcal{A}}\theta,\nabla_{\mathcal{A}}w)_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}+\int_{\Sigma}\theta w|\mathcal{N}|=\int_{\Omega}F^{8}\cdot wJ+\int_{\Sigma}F^{9}w

for each
w\in\mathcal{H}^{1}(t)
. By the elliptic theory,

(3.36)

\displaystyle\|\theta\|_{W^{2,q_{+}}}^{2}\lesssim\|\partial_{t}\theta\|_{H^{0}}^{2}+\|\theta\|_{H^{1}}^{2}+\|F^{8}\|_{L^{q_{+}}}^{2}+\|F^{9}\|_{W^{1-1/q_{+},q_{+}}}^{2}.

By (
3.24
), (
3.32
), (
3.34
) and (
3.36
), we have

(3.37)

\displaystyle\|\theta\|_{L^{2}W^{2,q_{+}}}^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\exp\{\|\partial_{t}\eta\|_{L^{\infty}H^{3/2+\varepsilon_{-}/2}}T\}\bigg(\|(F^{8}+F^{9})(0)\|_{(H^{1})^{\ast}}^{2}+\|\theta_{0}\|_{W^{2,q_{+}}(\Omega)}

\displaystyle+\|\partial_{t}\theta(0)\|_{H^{1+\varepsilon_{-}/2}}+(1+\|\partial_{t}\eta\|_{L^{\infty}H^{3/2+\varepsilon_{-}/2}}^{2})(\|F^{8}\|_{L^{2}L^{q_{+}}}^{2}+\|F^{9}\|_{L^{2}W^{1-1/q_{+},q_{+}}}^{2})

\displaystyle+(1+\|\partial_{t}\eta\|_{L^{\infty}H^{3/2+\varepsilon_{-}/2}}^{2})\|\partial_{t}(F^{8}+F^{9})\|_{(\mathcal{H}^{1}_{T})^{\ast}}^{2}\bigg),

as well as

(3.38)

\displaystyle\|\theta\|_{L^{\infty}W^{2,q_{+}}}^{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\exp\{\|\partial_{t}\eta\|_{L^{\infty}H^{3/2+\varepsilon_{-}/2}}T\}\bigg(\|\theta_{0}\|_{W^{2,q_{+}}(\Omega)}+\|\partial_{t}\theta(0)\|_{H^{1+\varepsilon_{-}/2}}+\|(F^{8}+F^{9})(0)\|_{(H^{1})^{\ast}}^{2}

\displaystyle+(1+\|\partial_{t}\eta\|_{L^{\infty}H^{3/2+\varepsilon_{-}/2}}^{2})(\|F^{8}\|_{L^{\infty}L^{q_{+}}}^{2}+\|F^{9}\|_{L^{\infty}W^{1-1/q_{+},q_{+}}}^{2}+\|F^{8}\|_{L^{2}L^{q_{+}}}^{2}

\displaystyle+\|F^{9}\|_{L^{2}W^{1-1/q_{+},q_{+}}}^{2})+(1+\|\partial_{t}\eta\|_{L^{\infty}H^{3/2+\varepsilon_{-}/2}}^{2})\|\partial_{t}(F^{8}+F^{9})\|_{(\mathcal{H}^{1}_{T})^{\ast}}^{2}\bigg).

Due to the bounds (
3.24
), (
3.32
), and (
3.37
),
\theta
have one more regularity, besides the weak solutions, so that
\theta
is a strong solution to (
1.19
).

Step 7 – Weak solution for
(\partial_{t}\theta)
.
Now we seek to use (
3.25
) to determine the PDE satisfied by
\partial_{t}\theta
.
We integrate (
3.25
) temporally over
[0,T]
and pass to the limit
m\rightarrow\infty
. The result equation is combined with the equation (
3.35
), with the test function
w\in L^{2}\mathcal{H}^{1}
, to derive that

(3.39)

\displaystyle\left<\partial_{t}^{2}\theta,w\right>_{\ast}+\int_{0}^{T}\left[(\nabla_{\mathcal{A}}\partial_{t}\theta,\nabla_{\mathcal{A}}w)_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}+(\partial_{t}\theta,w|\mathcal{N}|)_{0,\Sigma}\right]

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}\left[\partial_{t}F^{8}\cdot w+\partial_{t}JKF^{8}\cdot w\right]J+\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Sigma}[\partial_{t}F^{9}w|\mathcal{N}|

\displaystyle-\int_{0}^{T}\int_{\Omega}\frac{k}{2}(\nabla_{\partial_{t}\mathcal{A}}\theta\cdot\nabla_{\mathcal{A}}w+\nabla_{\mathcal{A}}\theta\cdot\nabla_{\partial_{t}\mathcal{A}}w+\partial_{t}JK\nabla_{\mathcal{A}}\theta\cdot\nabla_{\mathcal{A}}w)J

\displaystyle-(\partial_{t}JK\partial_{t}\theta,w)_{\mathcal{H}^{0}_{T}}-\int_{0}^{T}(\theta,w\partial_{t}|\mathcal{N}|)_{0,\Sigma}.

Then the definition of
R
that
R^{T}=-\partial_{t}M^{T}(M)^{-T}=-\partial_{t}JKI_{2\times 2}-\partial_{t}\mathcal{A}\mathcal{A}^{-1}
,
and integration by parts yield that

(3.40)

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}\frac{k}{2}(\nabla_{\partial_{t}\mathcal{A}}\theta\cdot\nabla_{\mathcal{A}}w+\nabla_{\mathcal{A}}\theta\cdot\nabla_{\partial_{t}\mathcal{A}}w+\partial_{t}JK\nabla_{\mathcal{A}}\theta\cdot\nabla_{\mathcal{A}}w)J

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega}k(\nabla_{\partial_{t}\mathcal{A}}\theta-R\nabla_{\mathcal{A}}\theta)\cdot\nabla_{\mathcal{A}}wJ

\displaystyle=-k\left(\mathop{\rm div}\nolimits_{\mathcal{A}}(\nabla_{\partial_{t}\mathcal{A}}\theta-R\nabla_{\mathcal{A}}\theta),w\right)_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}+k\left<\nabla_{\partial_{t}\mathcal{A}}\theta\cdot\mathcal{N}+\nabla_{\mathcal{A}}\theta\cdot\partial_{t}\mathcal{N},w\right>_{-1/2,\Sigma}.

Due to
R^{T}=-\partial_{t}JKI_{2\times 2}-\partial_{t}\mathcal{A}\mathcal{A}^{-1}
and
\partial_{j}(J\mathcal{A}_{ij})=0
, we also have

(3.41)

\displaystyle(\mathop{\rm div}\nolimits_{\mathcal{A}}R\nabla_{\mathcal{A}}\theta,w)_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}=(-\partial_{t}JK\Delta_{\mathcal{A}}\theta,w)_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}+(-\mathop{\rm div}\nolimits_{\partial_{t}\mathcal{A}}\nabla_{\mathcal{A}}\theta,w)_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}.

By the above inequalities (
3.39
)–(
3.41
), we have that

(3.42)

\displaystyle\left<\partial_{t}^{2}\theta,w\right>_{\ast}+k\int_{0}^{T}(\nabla_{\mathcal{A}}\partial_{t}\theta,\nabla_{\mathcal{A}}w)_{\mathcal{H}^{0}}+\int_{0}^{T}(\partial_{t}\theta,w|\mathcal{N}|)_{0,\Sigma}
